# Econometrics with Pre-Trained Embeddings for Unstructured Data

**arXiv ID**: 2607.17378v1
**Authors**: Yuya Shimizu
**Published**: 2026-07-19
**Categories**: econ.EM, math.ST, stat.ML
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.17378v1

## Abstract

Unstructured data, such as images and text, are increasingly used in empirical economics. Since training machine-learning models on unstructured data is costly, economists often use off-the-shelf pre-trained deep learning models developed by computer scientists to extract embeddings, which are then used as covariates in target economic analyses. Despite the popularity of this practice, its theoretical foundations remain limited. There are two main difficulties. First, the pre-trained model is usually trained on a different dataset and for a different task. Consequently, it is unclear when such a model can be used reliably for the target task. Second, the embedding function is subject to an identification problem, which makes it difficult to analyze the estimation error of the embedding function and its effect on the target task. In this paper, we provide sufficient conditions to overcome these difficulties and derive the convergence rate of machine learning models with pre-trained embeddings. We illustrate the theory through double machine learning applications for estimating parameters of interest, such as partially linear regression with unstructured controls, price elasticity in demand estimation considering the product quality measured by images and text, missing data imputation with unstructured data, and the average treatment effect with unstructured confounders.

## Abstract

Unstructured data, such as images and text, are increasingly used in empirical economics. Since training machine-learning models on unstructured data is costly, economists often use off-the-shelf pre-trained deep learning models developed by computer scientists to extract embeddings, which are then used as covariates in target economic analyses.
Despite the popularity of this practice, its theoretical foundations remain limited.
There are two main difficulties. First, the pre-trained model is usually trained on a different dataset and for a different task. Consequently, it is unclear when such a model can be used reliably for the target task. Second, the embedding function is subject to an identification problem, which makes it difficult to analyze the estimation error of the embedding function and its effect on the target task.
In this paper, we provide sufficient conditions to overcome these difficulties and derive the convergence rate of machine learning models with pre-trained embeddings.
We illustrate the theory through double machine learning applications for estimating parameters of interest, such as partially linear regression with unstructured controls, price elasticity in demand estimation considering the product quality measured by images and text, missing data imputation with unstructured data, and the average treatment effect with unstructured confounders.

Keywords: transfer learning, double machine learning, deep learning, average treatment effect, demand estimation

JEL codes: C45, C14, C55, C21

## 1Introduction

Unstructured data, such as images and text, are increasingly used in empirical economics.
In these applications, economists often use off-the-shelf pre-trained deep neural network models trained by computer scientists to extract embeddings and then use those embeddings as generated covariates in a downstream target task.222In the machine learning literature, embeddings are also commonly referred to as extracted features or learned representations.Embeddings transform unstructured data into structured numerical representations, typically vectors in a lower-dimensional latent space, that preserve relevant information from the original data.
From an econometric perspective, this is a two-step estimation problem (Murphy and Topel,1985; Pagan,1984).
This paper provides sufficient conditions under which this workflow yields valid inference in econometric analyses.

The structured dimensionality reduction can be substantial. A color image is typically represented by the intensity values of its pixels. For example, an image with resolution224×224224\times 224and three RGB color channels has224×224⏟pixels×3⏟RGB channels=150,528\underbrace{224\times 224}_{\text{pixels}}\times\underbrace{3}_{\text{RGB channels}}=150{,}528

raw input values.
Similarly, a text document is typically represented as a sequence of tokens drawn from a large vocabulary, corresponding to a vector with more than30,00030{,}000dimensions.
These raw vectors are ultra-high-dimensional and lack direct economic interpretation in the sense that individual pixel values or naive aggregations of token vectors generally do not correspond to economically meaningful variables.
This makes them difficult to use directly in standard empirical specifications, motivating the use of embeddings as lower-dimensional, structured representations of the same underlying information.

Pre-trained embeddings are increasingly used in empirical economics. In some applications, including embeddings can materially change estimates of parameters of interest, including their sign and statistical significance, relative to specifications that omit them.333For example,Avivi (2025)uses patent-text embeddings to control application quality when studying gender bias in patent examination.Dubeet al.(2020)estimates the elasticity of task vacancy-filling speed with respect to rewards, using task-description embeddings as controls.Bajariet al.(2025),Bachet al.(2024), andCompianiet al.(2025)use product text and image embeddings to control for product quality in hedonic regression and demand estimation.Klaassenet al.(2024)study causal effects with multimodal data.
Across these applications, the common assumption is that embeddings summarize economically relevant information in otherwise unstructured inputs and can be used as covariates to control or to impute missing variables.
Other applications use product embeddings to measure similarity in product space (Magnolfiet al.,2025; Han and Lee,2025) and satellite image embeddings to predict local poverty status (Jeanet al.,2016).Since training such representation models from scratch typically requires an extremely large sample and substantial computational resources, economists often rely on pre-trained embeddings as structured summaries of raw inputs.

Despite the empirical popularity of this workflow, its theoretical foundations remain limited.
There are two main theoretical difficulties.
First, the pre-trained model is usually trained on a different dataset and for a different task than the economic application; thus, it is unclear whether the pre-trained representation is valid for the target task.
Second, the embedding function is usually not identified: for example, we can insert a matrix and its inverse between the last hidden layer of a deep neural network and the output layer without changing the output.

To address these difficulties, we first formalize the setup containing a source task for the computer scientist and a target task for the economist. The embedding function is estimated from the source task and then used in the target task. In practice, the economist can access the pre-trained embedding function from the computer scientist via standard deep learning libraries such as PyTorch, but it is difficult or impossible for the economist to observe the source sample used to train the embedding function.
Our theory allows for this practical setting by treating the source sample as unobserved for the economist.444In the machine learning literature, this is known as a transfer-learning setup in which knowledge learned in the source task is reused in the target task (Pan and Yang,2010).

We then show that two conditions are key to justifying the two-step workflow of using pre-trained embeddings in the target task.
First, we assume that the source task and the target task share the same approximately true embedding function by requiring that approximation errors of both tasks are small when a shared embedding function is used for both. This condition formalizes the common (implicit) assumption in the empirical literature that the pre-trained embedding function summarizes the relevant information in the unstructured data for the target task.
Second, we impose a transferability condition requiring that a neighborhood of the source task identified set is contained in the corresponding target task neighborhood.
If the embedding function is the last hidden layer of a deep neural network and the economist uses a linear model on top of it, the transferability condition requires that the coefficient of the target task lies in the linear span of the source-task coefficients associated with the embedding function in population.

To illustrate the importance of the transferability condition, we compare a partially linear model with unstructured controls under two designs inFigure˜1, where one design has the target nuisance lying in the span of the source tasks and the other design does not.
The in-span design produces an estimator centered near the true parameter. By contrast, the out-of-span design exhibits a clear shift, motivating a transferability condition that rules out such non-transferable directions. SeeAppendix˜Ifor details of the simulation.Figure 1:Histogram ofθ^\widehat{\theta}for the in-span and out-of-span designs.

Under these conditions, we show that the convergence rate of transfer learning can be faster thann−1/4n^{-1/4}, which is sufficient for valid inference in the double machine learning (DML) framework ofChernozhukovet al.(2018)for a size-nntarget sample.
Our convergence rate accounts for randomness in both the source and target samples and depends jointly on the complexities and sample sizes of the source task and the target task.
The convergence rate in the target task is determined by the slower of the source-side and target-side rates.

Our theory also provides practical guidance for applied researchers.
First, the transferability condition is easier to justify for later hidden layers of a deep neural network than for earlier hidden layers, because earlier hidden layers introduce more complex partial identification issues. Thus, the last hidden layer is preferable as the embedding function if the economist is otherwise indifferent between earlier and last hidden layers.
Second, even under the sufficient conditions we impose and when the last hidden layer is selected as the embedding function, the embedding function is still not point identified, and it is only identified up to an invertible linear transformation.
Partial identification of the embedding function makes variable selection, such as LASSO, fragile. Instead, we recommend using ridge regression or shallow neural networks for the target model since they are robust to invertible linear transformations of the embeddings.

## Related Literature

The literature most closely related to ours can be grouped into three strands.
First, our paper contributes to the growing literature on inference with variables generated from unstructured data.Fong and Tyler (2021),Angelopouloset al.(2023),Egamiet al.(2023),Zhanget al.(2026),Battagliaet al.(2025),Carlson and Dell (2025), andLudwiget al.(2026)provide a framework to correct bias arising from variables generated from unstructured data in the context of missing data.
They typically assume a missing completely at random (MCAR) condition or a known propensity score.555Except forBattagliaet al.(2025), these papers use a missing-variable-imputation framework to handle missing data in the context ofChenet al.(2008).As discussed byCarlson and Dell (2025), the known propensity score assumption can be relaxed if we can estimate it with sufficiently fast convergence rates.
However, the convergence rate of propensity-score estimators based on unstructured data is not studied in these papers.
Beyond missing variable imputation,Rambachanet al.(2024),Modarressiet al.(2025), andCarlson (2025)study settings in which unstructured data are used to construct outcome measures.
Our paper departs from this literature by studying a general double machine learning setting in which generated embeddings enter the nuisance learning stage as inputs for both outcome imputation and propensity score estimation.

Second, several recent papers use pre-trained embeddings with theoretical justifications for downstream inference.Vafaet al.(2025)study wage-gap decomposition with foundation models.Bachet al.(2024)show that multimodal embeddings from text and images can improve demand estimation and elasticity analysis.
These two papers establish square-root-nnconsistency for the parameter of interest under high-level conditions.Christensen and Compiani (2026)develop a bias correction in demand counterfactuals when product differentiation is proxied by finite-dimensional nuisance parameters reparameterized from the embedding function and economic parameters.666Such reparameterization is not available for the general DML setup we consider in this paper, which includesExamples1,2,3and4.While the scope ofChristensen and Compiani (2026)is different from ours, their theory takes convergence of the nuisance parameters as given, and our theory can complement their theory by providing sufficient conditions for the convergence in probability.
The closest paper to ours isSchulteet al.(2025). They study the estimation of the average treatment effect (ATE) with unstructured confounders and point out the identification issues of the embedding function.
However, they ignore source-task estimation error and assume transferability across tasks implicitly.
Our paper differs by deriving a general convergence rate theory under explicit transferability conditions and by incorporating source task estimation error. This source task error is essential for valid target task inference.

Third, our transferability condition is related to the transfer learning literature in machine learning.Tripuraneniet al.(2020)introduce task diversity as a key condition for learning a shared representation that transfers well to new tasks, andWatkinset al.(2023)derive optimistic excess-risk bounds for multi-task representation learning under related diversity conditions.
The rate inWatkinset al.(2023)is comparable to our rate, but their theory requires a realizability condition that the risk of the true model is zero, which is hard to satisfy in economic applications.
Our paper uses a similar task diversity condition to ensure that the source task representation is valid for the target task, but we provide an econometric characterization of the condition through an identification lens.
Moreover, we provide an−1/4n^{-1/4}convergence rate for the estimated embedding function, which is directly applicable to econometric inference in the target task. In contrast, the machine learning literature typically studies excess-risk bounds for prediction and often obtains slower convergence rates for the estimated embedding function.

## 2Setup

We first introduce the general DML setup, then four empirical applications, and finally formalize the transfer learning setup for the machine learning components.
In the following examples, we consider the setting where the economist has a sample of sizennfor the target task and the computer scientist provides the pre-trained embeddingshwidecheck​(⋅)\widecheck{h}(\cdot)estimated independently from a different source task.
SeeSection˜2.3for the detailed discussion of the embedding functions.

## 2.1Double Machine Learning (DML)

Letψ​(Wi;θ,γ,α)\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma,\alpha)be a moment function, whereWiW_{i}is an observation from an i.i.d. sample,θ\thetais the parameter of interest, and(γ,α)(\gamma,\alpha)is an infinite-dimensional nuisance parameter vector.
We consider the moment condition:𝔼​[ψ​(Wi;θ∗,γ∗,α∗)]=0,\mathbb{E}[\psi(W_{i};\theta^{*},\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})]=0,(1)

whereθ∗\theta^{*}is the true value ofθ\theta, and(γ∗,α∗)(\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})is the true value of(γ,α)(\gamma,\alpha).
We assume thatγ∗\gamma^{*}andα∗\alpha^{*}are unknown functions ofZiZ_{i}, which is a subvector ofWiW_{i}containing unstructured or ultra-high-dimensional variables such as images and text.
The remaining components ofWiW_{i}are low-dimensional and structured, such as an outcome variable and a treatment variable.

We assume that the computer scientist provides a pre-trained embedding functionhwidecheck​(z)\widecheck{h}(z)estimated from a different source task.
In this setting, it is common for the economist to estimate the nuisance parameters(γ∗,α∗)(\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})using the pre-trained embeddingshwidecheck​(Zi)\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})as regressors in the target stage models.
Our nuisance parameter estimators forγ∗\gamma^{*}andα∗\alpha^{*}aref^γ∘hwidecheck​(z)\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\widecheck{h}(z)andf^α∘hwidecheck​(z)\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\circ\widecheck{h}(z), respectively, wheref^γ\widehat{f}_{\gamma}andf^α\widehat{f}_{\alpha}are the estimated target stage models using the pre-trained embeddingshwidecheck​(Zi)\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})as regressors.
Here,f^γ∘hwidecheck​(z)\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\widecheck{h}(z)is the composite function off^γ\widehat{f}_{\gamma}andhwidecheck​(z)\widecheck{h}(z), defined asf^γ∘hwidecheck​(z)=f^γ​(hwidecheck​(z))\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\widecheck{h}(z)=\widehat{f}_{\gamma}(\widecheck{h}(z)).
The DML estimatorθ^\widehat{\theta}is the solution of the sample moment equation1n​∑i=1nψ​(Wi;θ^,f^γ∘hwidecheck,f^α∘hwidecheck)=0.\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(W_{i};\widehat{\theta},\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\widecheck{h},\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\circ\widecheck{h})=0.(2)

To use the DML theory, we typically verify three key conditions: (i) Neyman orthogonality with respect to(γ,α)(\gamma,\alpha),777Escanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026)study locally robust estimation with generated regressors.
While their theory suggests that the generated regressors affect inference on the parameter of interest in general, this effect of generated regressors does not arise in our setup.
We discuss the relationship to their results inSectionA.2.(ii) sample splitting, and (iii) the sufficiently fast convergence rate of the nuisance parameters:888We implicitly assume that the Neyman orthogonal score function is smooth enough to use a second-order Taylor expansion. See also Assumption 3.2 inChernozhukovet al.(2018)and the discussion after that.‖γ∗​(Z)−f^γ∘hwidecheck​(Z)‖P,2=oP​(n−1/4)and‖α∗​(Z)−f^α∘hwidecheck​(Z)‖P,2=oP​(n−1/4),\|\gamma^{*}(Z)-\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z)\|_{P,2}=o_{P}(n^{-1/4})\quad\text{and}\quad\|\alpha^{*}(Z)-\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z)\|_{P,2}=o_{P}(n^{-1/4}),(3)

where∥⋅∥P,2\|\cdot\|_{P,2}is theL2​(P)L^{2}(P)norm for the distribution ofWiW_{i}, and theoPo_{P}notation is with respect to the source and target samples used to estimate(f^γ,f^α)(\widehat{f}_{\gamma},\widehat{f}_{\alpha})andhwidecheck\widecheck{h}.

The first condition holds by construction of the score, and the second holds by the sample-splitting procedure. Because the source sample is independent of the target sample and is used only to estimate nuisance parameters, the sample-splitting condition holds once the target sample is split between nuisance estimation and parameter estimation (Figure˜2).Figure 2:Sample Splitting with Transfer Learning

Alternatively, for some machine learning models, we can use the leave-one-out stability condition inChenet al.(2022)to avoid sample splitting.
Since the remaining DML arguments are the same as inChernozhukovet al.(2018), we omit the details.
Under additional regularity conditions on the moment function inChernozhukovet al.(2018), the asymptotic normality and consistency of the asymptotic variance estimator are guaranteed. Thus, this paper focuses on the third condition: the convergence rate of the nuisance parameters.

## Remark 1.

We implicitly assume that the two target stage machine learning modelsfγf_{\gamma}andfαf_{\alpha}use the same embedding functionhhfrom the source task for notational simplicity.
However, we can easily extend our theory to the case where two target stage machine learning models use different embeddingshγh_{\gamma}andhαh_{\alpha}from different source tasks.

## 2.2Applications

We include four empirical applications to illustrate the DML setup with pre-trained embeddings. Our theory can cover any DML model having the same structure as these examples.

## Example 1(Partially Linear Model with Unstructured Controls.).

We consider the partially linear modelYi=Xi​θ∗+κ∗​(Zi)+UiY_{i}=X_{i}\theta^{*}+\kappa^{*}(Z_{i})+U_{i}with𝔼​[Ui∣Xi,Zi]=0\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid X_{i},Z_{i}]=0,
whereYiY_{i}is the outcome,XiX_{i}is a scalar variable,ZiZ_{i}is a vector of unstructured or ultra-high-dimensional control variables such as images and text,κ∗​(⋅)\kappa^{*}(\cdot)is an unknown nonparametric function, andUiU_{i}is the error term.
The parameter of interest is the coefficientθ∗\theta^{*}ofXiX_{i}.
This is the classical partially linear setup (Robinson,1988), now with controls represented by unstructured-data embeddings.
We assume that an economist has a sample of sizenn,{(Yi,Xi,Zi)}i=1n\{(Y_{i},X_{i},Z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}, for the target task.
The parameterθ∗\theta^{*}is estimated by solving the Neyman orthogonal score𝔼​[ψ​(Wi;θ,γ∗,α∗)]=0\mathbb{E}[\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})]=0,
whereWi=(Yi,Xi,Zi)W_{i}=(Y_{i},X_{i},Z_{i}),ψ​(Wi;θ,γ,α)={(Yi−γ​(Zi))−(Xi−α​(Zi))​θ}​(Xi−α​(Zi))\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma,\alpha)=\{(Y_{i}-\gamma(Z_{i}))-(X_{i}-\alpha(Z_{i}))\theta\}(X_{i}-\alpha(Z_{i})),γ∗​(Zi)=𝔼​[Yi|Zi]\gamma^{*}(Z_{i})=\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|Z_{i}], andα∗​(Zi)=𝔼​[Xi|Zi]\alpha^{*}(Z_{i})=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}|Z_{i}].999Alternatively, we can use the scoreψ​(Wi;θ,κ,α)=(Yi−Xi​θ−κ​(Zi))​(Xi−α​(Zi))\psi(W_{i};\theta,\kappa,\alpha)=(Y_{i}-X_{i}\theta-\kappa(Z_{i}))(X_{i}-\alpha(Z_{i}))with the same nuisance parameters.Chernozhukovet al.(2018)show that both scores are Neyman orthogonal.The DML estimator is given byθ^=(1n​∑i=1n(Xi−f^α∘hwidecheck​(Zi))2)−1​(1n​∑i=1n(Xi−f^α∘hwidecheck​(Zi))​(Yi−f^γ∘hwidecheck​(Zi))).\widehat{\theta}=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})\right)^{2}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\widehat{f}_{\alpha}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})\right)\left(Y_{i}-\widehat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})\right)\right).(4)

## Example 2(Demand Estimation with Text and Image Data of Products).

Consider a variant of the demand estimation model inBerry (1994), motivated by the recent findings byBajariet al.(2025)andCompianiet al.(2025)that text and image embeddings of products are informative about consumer choice. Letθ∗\theta^{*}be the parameter of interest, which is the price elasticity.
LetYiY_{i}be the logarithm of the relative market share of productii,XiX_{i}be the price of productii, andZiZ_{i}be the unstructured or ultra-high-dimensional control variables of productii, such as product images and descriptions.
A simple demand estimation model is given byYi=Xi​θ∗+κ∗​(Zi)+UiY_{i}=X_{i}\theta^{*}+\kappa^{*}(Z_{i})+U_{i}and𝔼​[Ui∣Xi,Zi]=0\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid X_{i},Z_{i}]=0,
whereκ∗​(Z)\kappa^{*}(Z)is an unknown nonparametric function andUiU_{i}is an error term.
Under the exogenous price assumption, we can use the partially linear model as inExample˜1to estimate the price elasticityθ∗\theta^{*}.
The exogenous price assumption can also be relaxed. Suppose that the priceXiX_{i}is endogenous due to the brand effect, and the economist observes an instrumental variableViV_{i}for the priceXiX_{i}, such as a cost shifter.
Consider the demand model given byYi=Xi​θ∗+γ∗​(Zi)+Ui,Y_{i}=X_{i}\theta^{*}+\gamma^{*}(Z_{i})+U_{i},𝔼​[Ui∣Zi,Vi]=0\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid Z_{i},V_{i}]=0,Vi=α∗​(Zi)+eiV_{i}=\alpha^{*}(Z_{i})+e_{i}, and𝔼​[ei∣Zi]=0\mathbb{E}[e_{i}\mid Z_{i}]=0.
The parameter of interestθ∗\theta^{*}is estimated as the solution of the Neyman orthogonal moment equation𝔼​[ψ​(Wi;θ,γ∗,α∗)]=0\mathbb{E}[\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})]=0,
whereWi=(Yi,Xi,Vi,Zi)W_{i}=(Y_{i},X_{i},V_{i},Z_{i}),ψ​(Wi;θ,γ,α)=(Yi−Xi​θ−γ​(Zi))​(Vi−α​(Zi))\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma,\alpha)=(Y_{i}-X_{i}\theta-\gamma(Z_{i}))(V_{i}-\alpha(Z_{i})), andα∗​(Zi)=𝔼​[Vi|Zi]\alpha^{*}(Z_{i})=\mathbb{E}[V_{i}|Z_{i}].

## Example 3(Imputation with Unstructured Data).

Suppose that an economist observes a sample of sizenn,{(Yi,Xi,Di,Zi)}i=1n\{(Y_{i},X_{i},D_{i},Z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}, and is interested in the regression coefficientθ∗\theta^{*}, which is the solution of the moment𝔼​[(Yi−Xi​θ)​Xi]=0\mathbb{E}[(Y_{i}-X_{i}\theta)X_{i}]=0, whereYiY_{i}is the observed outcome andXiX_{i}is a scalar covariate with missing values.XiX_{i}is observed ifDi=1D_{i}=1and missing ifDi=0D_{i}=0.
The economist can use the unstructured or ultra-high-dimensional variablesZiZ_{i}to predictXiX_{i}.
We assume thatXiX_{i}is missing at random, i.e.,Xi⟂⟂Di∣ZiX_{i}\mathrel{\perp\mspace{-10.0mu}\perp}D_{i}\mid Z_{i}(Rubin,1976).
The parameterθ∗\theta^{*}is estimated by solving the Neyman orthogonal moment equation𝔼​[ψ​(Wi;θ,γ∗,α∗)]=0\mathbb{E}[\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})]=0,
whereWi=(Yi,Xi,Di,Zi)W_{i}=(Y_{i},X_{i},D_{i},Z_{i}),ψ​(Wi;θ,γ,α)=(Di/α​(Zi))​(Yi​Xi−Xi2​θ−(γ1​(Zi)−γ2​(Zi)​θ))+γ1​(Zi)−γ2​(Zi)​θ\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma,\alpha)=(D_{i}/\alpha(Z_{i}))(Y_{i}X_{i}-X_{i}^{2}\theta-(\gamma_{1}(Z_{i})-\gamma_{2}(Z_{i})\theta))+\gamma_{1}(Z_{i})-\gamma_{2}(Z_{i})\theta,γ1∗​(Zi)=𝔼​[Xi​Yi|Zi]\gamma^{*}_{1}(Z_{i})=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}Y_{i}|Z_{i}],γ2∗​(Zi)=𝔼​[Xi2|Zi]\gamma^{*}_{2}(Z_{i})=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}|Z_{i}],γ∗=(γ1∗,γ2∗)\gamma^{*}=(\gamma_{1}^{*},\gamma_{2}^{*})andα∗​(Zi)=P​[Di=1|Zi]\alpha^{*}(Z_{i})=P[D_{i}=1|Z_{i}]is the propensity score of observingXiX_{i}.

## Example 4(Average Treatment Effect with Unstructured confounders).

Under the unconfoundedness assumptionYi⟂⟂Di∣ZiY_{i}\mathrel{\perp\mspace{-10.0mu}\perp}D_{i}\mid Z_{i}, the average treatment effect (ATE) is identified byθ∗=𝔼​[γ∗​(1,Zi)−γ∗​(0,Zi)]\theta^{*}=\mathbb{E}[\gamma^{*}(1,Z_{i})-\gamma^{*}(0,Z_{i})],
whereYiY_{i}is the outcome,DiD_{i}is the binary treatment,ZiZ_{i}denotes the unstructured or ultra-high-dimensional confounders, andγ∗​(d,Zi)=𝔼​[Yi∣Di=d,Zi]\gamma^{*}(d,Z_{i})=\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mid D_{i}=d,Z_{i}](Rosenbaum and Rubin,1983).
The ATE is estimated using DML with the Neyman orthogonal moment equationψ​(Wi;θ,γ∗,α∗)=0\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*})=0,
whereWi=(Yi,Di,Zi)W_{i}=(Y_{i},D_{i},Z_{i}),ψ​(Wi;θ,γ,α)=(Di​(Yi−γ​(1,Zi))/(α​(Zi))−(1−Di)​(Yi−γ​(0,Zi))/(1−α​(Zi)))+(γ​(1,Zi)−γ​(0,Zi)−θ)\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma,\alpha)=(D_{i}(Y_{i}-\gamma(1,Z_{i}))/(\alpha(Z_{i}))-(1-D_{i})(Y_{i}-\gamma(0,Z_{i}))/(1-\alpha(Z_{i})))+(\gamma(1,Z_{i})-\gamma(0,Z_{i})-\theta), andα∗​(Zi)=P​[Di=1|Zi]\alpha^{*}(Z_{i})=P[D_{i}=1|Z_{i}]is the propensity score of receiving treatment.

## 2.3Transfer Learning

In this subsection, we explain the transfer-learning setup for the DML estimator.
We explicitly consider the source task for the computer scientist, together with the target task for the economist.

Hereafter, letPsoP_{\operatorname{so}}andPtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}denote the source and target task distributions, and let𝔼so\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{so}}and𝔼ta\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}denote the corresponding expectations.
LetPPand𝔼\mathbb{E}denote the joint distribution of the source and target tasks and its expectation, respectively.
Thus, the expectations and probabilities used in the above subsections are under the target task𝔼ta\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}andPtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}except for theoPo_{P}notation, which is with respect to both the source and target tasks.

We consider the setting where a source sample of sizemmand a target sample of sizennshare the same approximately true embedding functionhmh_{m}(Definition˜1).
Our asymptotics let both the source sample sizemmand the target sample sizenngo to infinity, where the rate at whichmmdiverges may depend onnn.
Formally, we treat the source sample sizem=mnm=m_{n}as a sequence indexed by the target sample sizennand letmn→∞m_{n}\to\inftyasn→∞n\to\infty.
Suppose that these samples are generated by(Sj,Zj)∼i.i.d.Pso(S_{j},Z_{j})\sim_{i.i.d.}P_{\operatorname{so}}forj=1,…,mj=1,\dots,mand(Yi,Zi)∼i.i.d.Pta(Y_{i},Z_{i})\sim_{i.i.d.}P_{\operatorname{ta}}fori=1,…,ni=1,\dots,n, where(Sj,Zj)(S_{j},Z_{j})and(Yi,Zi)(Y_{i},Z_{i})are independent andZjZ_{j}andZiZ_{i}have different marginal probability distributions.
Here,Sj∈𝒮⊂ℝS_{j}\in\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}is the outcome for the source task,Yi∈𝒴⊂ℝY_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}is the outcome for the target task, andZj,Zi∈𝒵⊂ℝdZ_{j},Z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}denote unstructured or ultra-high-dimensional covariates.

## Source Task for Computer Scientist

We focus on the most empirically relevant setting where the source task outcome is multi-valued, i.e.,𝒮={1,2,…,T+1}\mathcal{S}=\{1,2,\dots,T+1\}for some integerT≥1T\geq 1.
Then, the source task is multi-class classification withT+1T+1classes and the computer scientist trains the pre-trained modelg∘h​(z):𝒵↦ℝTg\circ h(z):\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{T}to provide the predicted log-odds for each classt=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,Tagainst the base classT+1T+1.
Leth:𝒵↦𝒱⊂ℝKh:\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}be an embedding function returningh​(z)∈𝒱h(z)\in\mathcal{V}for some integerK≥1K\geq 1, and letg=(g1,…,gT):𝒱↦ℝTg=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{T}):\mathcal{V}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{T}be a model for the source task, wheregt​(v)g_{t}(v)is thett-th element ofg​(v)g(v)fort=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,T. We normalize the score of the base class asgT+1​(v)=0g_{T+1}(v)=0for everyv∈𝒱v\in\mathcal{V}.
Let𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}denote the function class ofgg,𝒢t,m\mathcal{G}_{t,m}denote the function class ofgtg_{t}for eacht=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,T, andℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}denote the function class ofhhfor the source task.
For example, in a deep neural network model, the model can be decomposed asg∘hg\circ h, wherehhis some hidden layer of a deep neural network andggis the remaining transformation from the hidden layer to the output layer.
For a given loss functionℓso:ℝT×𝒮↦ℝ\ell_{\operatorname{so}}:\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R}, let the set of population minimizers be the solution of the population risk minimization:101010As inFarrellet al.(2021), we assume that minimizer(s) exist for both population and sample risk for simplicity. For the statistical convergence rate theorem, exact existence is not crucial as we can always replace the infimum by a function within a functional class with arbitrarily close risk.ℛm=arg​ming∈𝒢m,h∈ℋm⁡𝔼so​[ℓso​(g∘h​(Z),S)].\mathcal{R}_{m}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m},h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{so}}[\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(g\circ h(Z),S)].(5)

For a score vectoraso=(aso,1,…,aso,T)′∈ℝTa_{\operatorname{so}}=(a_{\operatorname{so},1},\ldots,a_{\operatorname{so},T})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}, we similarly normalize the base-class score asaso,T+1=0a_{\operatorname{so},T+1}=0. The most common loss function for multi-class classification is the multinomial logit loss:111111This loss function is also common in the machine learning literature, where it is often called the cross-entropy loss with a softmax output layer.ℓso​(aso,S)=−∑t=1T𝟙​{S=t}​aso,t+log⁡(1+∑t=1Texp⁡(aso,t)).\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(a_{\operatorname{so}},S)=-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathds{1}\{S=t\}a_{\operatorname{so},t}+\log\left(1+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\exp(a_{\operatorname{so},t})\right).

The key observation here is that the source task is essentially trained onTTdifferent tasksg1,…,gTg_{1},\ldots,g_{T}with shared embeddingsh​(Z)∈ℝKh(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}to classify labels withT+1T+1classes.

The computer scientist observes a sample of sizemm,{(Sj,Zj)}j=1m∼Psom\{(S_{j},Z_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}\sim P_{\operatorname{so}}^{m}, for the source task and estimates the pre-trained model(gwidecheck,hwidecheck)(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{h})by some estimation method, such as empirical risk minimization:(gwidecheck,hwidecheck)∈arg​ming∈𝒢m,h∈ℋm⁡1m​∑j=1mℓso​(g∘h​(Zj),Sj).(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{h})\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m},h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(g\circ h(Z_{j}),S_{j}).(6)

## Target Task for Economist

Next, we consider the target task for the economist.
The economist will train the target modelf∘h​(z):𝒵↦ℝf\circ h(z):\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\mathbb{R}to predict the target outcomeY∈𝒴Y\in\mathcal{Y}using learned embeddings from the source task.
Letf:𝒱↦ℝf:\mathcal{V}\mapsto\mathbb{R}be the model for the target task, whereℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}is the function class offffor the target task.
Letℓta:ℝ×𝒴↦ℝ\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\mathbb{R}be the loss function for the target task such as the least squares loss for regression and the logistic loss for binary classification.
Given an embedding functionhmh_{m}from the source task, letℱnsub​(hm)\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m})denote the set of solutions to the population risk minimization problem:ℱnsub​(hm)=arg​minf∈ℱnsub⁡𝔼ta​[ℓta​(f∘hm​(Z),Y)],\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m})=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(f\circ h_{m}(Z),Y)],(7)

whereℱnsub⊆ℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{n}is a subset of a functional classℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}for the target task, possibly subject to restrictions such as a sparsity restriction for LASSO in a linear modelℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}.
We often haveℱnsub=ℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}=\mathcal{F}_{n}without additional restrictions, for example for linear regression or shallow neural networks.

The economist observes a sample of sizenn,{Yi,Zi}i=1n∼Ptan\{Y_{i},Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim P_{\operatorname{ta}}^{n}, and knows the pre-trained modelhwidecheck\widecheck{h}given by the computer scientist, but does not observe the source sample.
Since the embeddinghwidecheck​(z)\widecheck{h}(z)is often high-dimensional, the economist can use various machine learning models forffin the target task.
For example, the economist uses a pre-trained convolutional neural network (CNN) to extract high-dimensional embeddingshwidecheck​(z)\widecheck{h}(z)(e.g., embeddings of dimension 4,096 from a final pooling layer) from ultra-high-dimensional image data (e.g., pixel information with 150,528 dimensions).
The extracted embeddings are then used as regressors, for example, in a ridge regression of the outcomeYiY_{i}onhwidecheck​(Zi)\widecheck{h}(Z_{i}).

Finally, the economist estimates the target modelf^\widehat{f}by empirical risk minimization:121212Throughout the paper, we impose suitable conditions ensuring that minimizers indexed by random variables exist and are measurable.f^∈arg​minf∈ℱn⁡1n​∑i=1nℓta​(f∘hwidecheck​(Zi),Yi).\widehat{f}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(f\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i}),Y_{i}).(8)

In our theory, we allow small optimization errors for both estimators as shown in˜8and6inTheorems˜6and7.

In summary, a computer scientist provides the pre-trained model(gwidecheck,hwidecheck)(\widecheck{g},\widecheck{h})estimated from the source sample{(Sj,Zj)}j=1m\{(S_{j},Z_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}, and an economist uses the embeddingshwidecheck​(Zi)\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})as regressors to estimate the target functionf^\widehat{f}in the target sample{(Yi,Zi)}i=1n\{(Y_{i},Z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}.

Below, we present four examples of the transfer learning setup.
Further details of pre-trained deep learning models are provided inAppendix˜B.

## Example 5(DNN Embeddings for Ultra-High-Dimensional Data).

Consider the setting where the computer scientist trains a deep neural network (DNN) on the source task and the economist uses the learned embeddings as inputs to machine learning models in the target DML task.
In this case, the embedding functionhhis given by the last hidden layer of the DNN, and the pre-trained modelggis given by the output layer of the DNN (linear regression). Economists can use various machine learning models, such as shallow neural networks and ridge regression, for the functionffin the target task.
The dataZZcan be ultra-high-dimensional, and the DNN can extract high-dimensional embeddings of the data using a source task nonparametric regression problem, where the outcomeSSis a continuous variable and the loss functionℓso\ell_{\operatorname{so}}is the mean squared error loss.

An autoencoder is a special case of the DNN inExample˜5, where the source task is the nonparametric regression task with the same input and output, i.e.,S=ZS=Z, and the loss functionℓso\ell_{\operatorname{so}}is a distance function between the input and output, such as the mean squared error loss (Hinton and Salakhutdinov,2006).

## Example 6(CNN Embeddings for Image Data).

The computer scientist trains a CNN, such as VGG19 (Simonyan and Zisserman,2015) or ResNet50 (Heet al.,2016), on the source task131313Pre-trained models for both CNNs are available in the torchvision package in PyTorch., and the economist uses the learned embeddings as inputs to machine learning models in the target DML task as inExample˜5.
The embedding functionhhis the last pooling layer of the CNN. The source headggis the classifier attached to that embedding: a three-hidden-layer network with a softmax output in VGG19, or a one-hidden-layer network with a softmax output in ResNet50.
The covariateZZis an image, and the CNN can extract high-dimensional embeddings of dimension 4,096 for VGG19 and 1,000 for ResNet50 from the image using the nonparametric classification task in the source task, where the outcomeSSis an image label (1,000 classes in ImageNet) and the loss functionℓso\ell_{\operatorname{so}}is the cross-entropy loss (negative log likelihood).

## Example 7(Transformer Embeddings for Text Data).

The computer scientist trains a transformer model, such as BERT (Devlinet al.,2019), on the source task141414Pre-trained models for BERT are available in the Hugging Face Transformers package in PyTorch., and the economist uses the learned embeddings as inputs to machine learning models in the target DML task as inExample˜5.
BERT is pre-trained on two tasks: masked language modeling and next sentence prediction.
The embedding functionh​(S,T)h(\texttt{S},\texttt{T})is given by the last output vectors of the transformer for the input sentenceSand tokenT.
The token is a masked token[MASK]in the masked language modeling task or the special token[CLS]in the next sentence prediction task.
To separate the sentences, the special token[SEP]is inserted between the first sentence and the second sentence.
The modelg1g_{1}is trained to predict the masked token using the masked language modeling task, and the modelg2g_{2}is trained to predict whether the second sentence follows the first sentence using the next sentence prediction task.
They are simply linear classifiers on the embeddingsh​(S,T)h(\texttt{S},\texttt{T}).
Both tasks use cross-entropy loss. The masked language modeling label is the original masked token (30,000-label classification), while the next-sentence prediction label is binary.
For the target task, economists can use various machine learning models.
The dataZZare text, and the transformer can extract high-dimensional embeddings of the text using source classification tasks.
To extract a text-level embedding of dimension 1,024 for the BERT LARGE model, we can use either the output vector for the special token[CLS], i.e.,h​(S,[CLS])h(\texttt{S},\texttt{[CLS]}), or the average of the output vectors for all tokens in the textS, i.e.,|S|−1​∑T∈Sh​(S,T)|\texttt{S}|^{-1}\sum_{\texttt{T}\in\texttt{S}}h(\texttt{S},\texttt{T}).151515Whereas the original BERT paper (Devlinet al.,2019) updates all parameters of the pre-trained model for the target task, we only consider the embeddings learned in the source task without updating the parameters of the pre-trained model. Both approaches are called fine-tuning in the machine learning literature. We use the term fine-tuning to mean updating all parameters of the pre-trained model.

SeeAppendix˜Bfor details of the functional form of the pre-trained models in the examples above.

## Example 8(Multimodal Embeddings for Image and Text Data).

The economist can also use the multimodal embeddings learned by the computer scientist.
For example, the economist can use images and text as unstructured inputs and extract embeddings separately using a pre-trained CNN and a pre-trained transformer.

## 3Transfer Learning Theory

This and the next sections present our main theoretical results on transfer learning for DML.
For this purpose, we first introduce the true models for the source and target tasks.
Suppose that the source and target tasks admit true models satisfyingaso∗∈arg​minaso:square-integrable⁡𝔼so​[ℓso​(aso​(Z),S)]a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{a_{\operatorname{so}}:\text{ square-integrable}}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{so}}[\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(a_{\operatorname{so}}(Z),S)]

andata∗∈arg​minata:square-integrable⁡𝔼ta​[ℓta​(ata​(Z),Y)],a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{a_{\operatorname{ta}}:\text{ square-integrable}}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z),Y)],

respectively.
For example, suppose that the source loss functionℓso\ell_{\operatorname{so}}is the multinomial logit loss,Pso​(S=t∣Z)>0P_{\operatorname{so}}(S=t\mid Z)>0for everyt=1,…,T+1t=1,\ldots,T+1,PsoP_{\operatorname{so}}-a.s., and the corresponding log-odds functions are square-integrable underPsoP_{\operatorname{so}}. Under the normalizationaso,T+1∗​(Z)=0a_{\operatorname{so},T+1}^{*}(Z)=0and suitable moment conditions, the true source model is almost surely unique and given by the log-odds of the class probabilities,aso,t∗​(Z)=log⁡(Pso​(S=t∣Z)/Pso​(S=T+1∣Z))a_{\operatorname{so},t}^{*}(Z)=\log(P_{\operatorname{so}}(S=t\mid Z)/P_{\operatorname{so}}(S=T+1\mid Z))fort=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,T.
For the target task, if the loss is the squared lossℓta​(ata​(Z),Y)=(1/2)×(Y−ata​(Z))2\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z),Y)=(1/2)\times(Y-a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z))^{2}and suitable moment conditions are satisfied, the true model is almost surely unique and given by the conditional expectation of the outcome asata∗​(Z)=𝔼ta​[Y∣Z]a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}(Z)=\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y\mid Z], and if the loss is the binary logit lossℓta​(ata​(Z),Y)=−Y​log⁡(exp⁡(ata​(Z))/(1+exp⁡(ata​(Z))))−(1−Y)​log⁡(1/(1+exp⁡(ata​(Z))))\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z),Y)=-Y\log(\exp(a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z))/(1+\exp(a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z))))-(1-Y)\log(1/(1+\exp(a_{\operatorname{ta}}(Z))))and suitable moment conditions are satisfied, the true model is almost surely unique and given by the log-odds of the conditional probability asata∗​(Z)=log⁡(Pta​(Y=1∣Z)/Pta​(Y=0∣Z))a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}(Z)=\log(P_{\operatorname{ta}}(Y=1\mid Z)/P_{\operatorname{ta}}(Y=0\mid Z)).
In DML applications, these two loss functions are the most common for the target task.

In this section, we develop a general convergence rate theory for the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}by relating it to the source task performance of the pre-trained modelgwidecheck∘hwidecheck\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}relative to the true modelaso∗a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}.
InSection˜3.1, we introduce two key concepts: an approximately true embedding function and task diversity.
InSection˜3.2, we present the theorem on the convergence rate of the target model in general form.Section˜3.3discusses sufficient and necessary conditions for task diversity.

## 3.1Key Concepts

## 3.1.1Approximately True Embedding Function

We first introduce the concept of an approximately true embedding function, which is a key definition for the transfer learning theory.
In general,aso∗a^{*}_{\operatorname{so}}andata∗a^{*}_{\operatorname{ta}}need not be representable asgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}andfn∘hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}.
This mismatch arises from restrictions on the source classes𝒢m,ℋm\mathcal{G}_{m},\mathcal{H}_{m}and the target classℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}even if there exists an embedding function that captures the common features of the source and target tasks well.
The following definition quantifies the approximation error for both source and target tasks.

## Definition 1(Approximation Error).

For sequences of functions(gm,hm)∈ℛm(g_{m},h_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{m}andfn∈ℱnsub​(hm)f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m}), we define the approximation errors for the source and target tasks as‖gm∘hm−aso∗‖Pso,2\displaystyle\|g_{m}\circ h_{m}-a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}=:ϵso,m,\displaystyle=:\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m},(9)‖fn∘hm−ata∗‖Pta,2\displaystyle\|f_{n}\circ h_{m}-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=:ϵta,n.\displaystyle=:\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}.(10)

## Remark 2.

This definition allowsϵso,m\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}andϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}to depend on(gm,hm)(g_{m},h_{m})and(fn,hm)(f_{n},h_{m}), respectively, but we suppress this dependence in the notation for simplicity.
If there exists a true embedding functionh∗h^{*}such thath∗∈ℋmh^{*}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}eventually and there exist some measurable functionsg∗g^{*}andf∗f^{*}such thataso∗=g∗∘h∗a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}=g^{*}\circ h^{*},ata∗=f∗∘h∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}=f^{*}\circ h^{*}, then we can interpreth∗h^{*}as the true embedding function andg∗g^{*}andf∗f^{*}as the true source and target models, respectively.
Although it is common to assume the existence of the true embedding function in the transfer learning literature and setϵso,m\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}andϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}exactly to zero, we allow the approximate embedding function to cover more general cases where there is no true embedding function, but the source and target models can be well approximated by some embedding function.

While we state it as a definition, our result requires that the approximation errorsϵso,m\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}andϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}converge to zero asn→∞n\to\inftyfor consistency of the DML estimator inTheorem˜6.
Decays ofϵso,m\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}andϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}are determined by the richness of the function classes𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}andℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}and how well the embedding functionhmh_{m}captures the common features of the source and target tasks. When the function classes𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}andℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}are linear, the approximation errorsϵso,m\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}andϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}are determined by the linear projection ofaso∗a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}andata∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}onto the linear span of the embedding functionhmh_{m}, which is similar to the approximation error in the series regression model (Newey,1997) using some basis functions instead of the embedding functionhmh_{m}.

## 3.1.2Task Diversity

Next, we introduce the concept of task diversity, which is another key definition.
We define task diversity in a way that makes its connection to identification explicit.
The reader can refer toSection˜A.1for a detailed comparison with existing definitions of task diversity in the machine learning literature.
For functions(gm,hm)∈ℛm(g_{m},h_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{m}andfn∈ℱnsub​(hm)f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m}), define the following approximate identified sets ofhmh_{m}forρ≥0\rho\geq 0:ℋso,m​(ρ):={h∈ℋm:ming∈𝒢m⁡‖g∘h−gm∘hm‖Pso,2≤ρ}\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho):=\left\{h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}:\min_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\left\|g\circ h-g_{m}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}\leq\rho\right\}

andℋta,m​(ρ):={h∈ℋm:minf∈ℱnsub⁡‖f∘h−fn∘hm‖Pta,2≤ρ},\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{ta},m}(\rho):=\left\{h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}:\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\left\|f\circ h-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}\leq\rho\right\},

where we assume that the minimum exists for both the source and target tasks for simplicity.
Note thatℋso,m​(0)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)andℋta,m​(0)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{ta},m}(0)are the identified sets ofhmh_{m}for the source task and the target task, respectively.
Ifhmh_{m}is identified, these sets shrink to{hm}\{h_{m}\}asρ↓0\rho\downarrow 0. In general, however,hmh_{m}need not be identified, and these sets can contain multiple elements. It is known that the hidden layers of the deep neural network are identified only up to some transformations, such as the permutation of the order of the hidden units, the scaling of the hidden units, and the rotation of the hidden units (Grigsbyet al.,2023). See alsoExample˜10for another example of partial identification of the embedding function for middle hidden layers of a deep neural network.

## Definition 2(Task Diversity).

Fix functions(gm,hm)∈ℛm(g_{m},h_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{m}andfn∈ℱnsub​(hm)f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m}).
For function classes𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m},ℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}, andℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}and someρso,m≥0\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\geq 0andρta,n≥0\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}\geq 0, we say thatgmg_{m}is(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}ifℋso,m​(ρso,m)⊆ℋta,m​(ρta,n).\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{ta},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}).(11)

Thus, the task diversity condition requires that ifhhis in a near-identified set of the source task, thenhhis also in a near-identified set of the target task, where the distances are measured by theL2​(Pso)L^{2}(P_{\operatorname{so}})- andL2​(Pta)L^{2}(P_{\operatorname{ta}})-norms, respectively.

The name “task diversity” comes from the intuition that if theTTsource tasks are sufficiently diverse, then any embedding functionhhclose to the source task identified set should also be close to the target task identified set.
This intuition and sufficient and necessary conditions for task diversity are formalized inSection˜3.3.

Next, we introduce the concept ofνn\nu_{n}-transferability, which is defined in terms of the task diversity condition.

## Definition 3(νn\nu_{n}-Transferability).

Fix a sequence{νn}n=1∞\{\nu_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}satisfyingνn>0\nu_{n}>0for everynn, sequences of functions(gm,hm)∈ℛm(g_{m},h_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{m}, andfn∈ℱnsub​(hm)f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m}).
For sequences of function classes{𝒢m}m=1∞\{\mathcal{G}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty},{ℋm}m=1∞\{\mathcal{H}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}, and{ℱnsub}n=1∞\{\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}, we say thatgmg_{m}isνn\nu_{n}-transferable tofnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}at rateδm\delta_{m}if for every sequence{ρso,m}m=1∞\{\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}such thatρso,m=O​(δm)\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}=O(\delta_{m}), the(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diversity condition holds withρta,n=ρso,m/νn\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}for all large enoughnn.

νn\nu_{n}-transferability at rateδm\delta_{m}requires that the(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diversity condition holds for all source-side sequencesρso,m=O​(δm)\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}=O(\delta_{m})withρta,n=ρso,m/νn\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}.
The quantityνn\nu_{n}captures the identification strength of the target task informed by the source task.
Largerνn\nu_{n}implies that the taskgmg_{m}is more transferable tofnf_{n}since it implies a smaller target task near-identified set given the near-identified set of the source task.

We will relate the sequencesρso,m\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}to the convergence rates of the source task introduced below.
Letfn∙∈arg​minf∈ℱnsub⁡‖f∘hwidecheck−fn∘hm‖Pta,2f_{n}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\|f\circ\widecheck{h}-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}be the best approximation of the population target modelfn∘hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}by the estimated embedding functionhwidecheck\widecheck{h}.
Formally,fn∙f_{n}^{\bullet}depends onhwidecheck\widecheck{h}, but we suppress the dependence for notational simplicity.

## Assumption 1(Convergence Rate of Models).
- (i)

(Source Model) There exists some sequenceδso,m≥0\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}\geq 0such that‖gwidecheck∘hwidecheck−gm∘hm‖Pso,2=OPso​(δso,m).\left\|\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}-g_{m}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}=O_{P_{\operatorname{so}}}(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}).(12)
- (ii)

(Target Model givenhwidecheck\widecheck{h}) For every sequence of estimated embedding functionshwidecheck∈ℋm\widecheck{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}satisfying˜12, there exists some sequencerta,n≥0r_{\operatorname{ta},n}\geq 0such that‖f^∘hwidecheck−fn∙∘hwidecheck‖Pta,2=OP​(rta,n).\left\|\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}-f_{n}^{\bullet}\circ\widecheck{h}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}(r_{\operatorname{ta},n}).(13)

The first part of this assumption states that the pre-trained modelgwidecheck∘hwidecheck\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}converges to the population source modelgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}at some rateδso,m\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}.
The second part states that the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}converges to the population target modelfn∙∘hwidecheckf_{n}^{\bullet}\circ\widecheck{h}at some raterta,nr_{\operatorname{ta},n}given the estimated embedding functionhwidecheck\widecheck{h}.
Note that theOPO_{P}notation in the second part is with respect to both the source and target samples sincehwidecheck\widecheck{h}depends on the source sample.
This assumption is high-level, and we will provide more primitive sufficient conditions inSection˜4to avoid assuming this directly.

Now, we assume theνn\nu_{n}-transferability condition with the convergence rate of the source taskδso,m\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}.

## Assumption 2(Transferability with Convergence Rates).

For sequences of function classes{𝒢m}m=1∞\{\mathcal{G}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty},{ℋm}m=1∞\{\mathcal{H}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}, and{ℱnsub}n=1∞\{\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}, there exist(gm,hm)∈ℛm(g_{m},h_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{m}andfn∈ℱnsub​(hm)f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m})such that the taskgmg_{m}isνn\nu_{n}-transferable tofnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}at rateδso,m\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}, whereδso,m\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}is the convergence rate of the source task defined inAssumption˜1.

Assumption˜2and the quantityνn\nu_{n}play a key role in determining the convergence rate of the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}as we will see in the next subsection.

## 3.2Convergence Rate of Target Model

Our main theorem on the convergence rate of the target model is as follows.

## Theorem 1(Convergence Rate of Target Model).

Suppose thatAssumptions˜1and2hold.
Then, for every sequence of estimated embedding functionsh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘∈ℋm\widecheck{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}satisfying˜12, we have‖f^∘hwidecheck−ata∗‖Pta,2=OP​(rta,n+δso,m/νn+ϵta,n).\left\|\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}-a^{*}_{\operatorname{ta}}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}\left(r_{\operatorname{ta},n}+\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}\right).(14)

This theorem states that the convergence rate of the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}to the true modelata∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}is determined by three components: (i) the convergence raterta,nr_{\operatorname{ta},n}of the target model given the estimated embedding functionhwidecheck\widecheck{h}, (ii) the convergence rate of the source task adjusted by the transferability strengthνn\nu_{n}, and (iii) the approximation errorϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}due to an approximately true embedding function.
Thus, the rate of the target model is determined by the slowest of these three components.
The target-stage raterta,nr_{\operatorname{ta},n}is achieved when transferability is strong, the source task converges sufficiently fast, and the approximation error is sufficiently small.

## Remark 3.

Applied work using source task embeddings often ignores the estimation error of the pre-trained model and directly applies the DML theory for the target task by treating the estimated embedding functionh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widecheck{h}as the approximately true embedding functionhmh_{m}.
This theorem provides a theoretical justification for this common practice by showing that the estimation error of the pre-trained model can be negligible if the transferability is strong, the source task converges sufficiently fast, and the approximation error is sufficiently small.
However, there are two important differences between the current practice and the theoretical result in this theorem.
First, current practice often ignores the randomness of the estimated embedding functionh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widecheck{h}, while our convergence rate is with respect to joint randomness underPP, rather than target sample randomness underPtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}alone.
Second, applied work often ignores nonidentification ofhmh_{m}and the associated task diversity condition. Without considering the nonidentification issue, the convergence rate can depend on a specific optimization algorithm used by the computer scientist to estimate the pre-trained model, which is not desirable for the economist.
The theorem addresses both issues by imposing transferability and by allowing any estimated embedding functionh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widecheck{h}in a source task near-identified set.161616Our rate is pointwise with respect to the sequence of estimated embedding functionsh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘∈ℋm\widecheck{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}satisfying12.
Extending the result to a uniform convergence rate over the estimated set of embedding functionshmh_{m}is an interesting direction for future research, but doing so would require additional assumptions and is beyond the scope of this paper.

The next theorem gives a useful necessary condition based on the source task identified setℋso,m​(0)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0). We will revisit this necessary condition inSection˜4.2.2for the specific case of the LASSO.

## Theorem 2(Necessary Condition for Convergence of Target Model).

Fix sequences of functions(gm,hm)∈ℛm(g_{m},h_{m})\in\mathcal{R}_{m}andfn∈ℱnsub​(hm)f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}(h_{m}).
Letfn,hm†∙∈arg​minf∈ℱnsub⁡‖f∘hm†−fn∘hm‖Pta,2f_{n,h_{m}^{\dagger}}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\|f\circ h_{m}^{\dagger}-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}be the best approximation of the population target modelfn∘hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}by the embedding functionhm†h_{m}^{\dagger}.
Letf^n,hm†∈arg​minf∈ℱnsub⁡1n​∑i=1nℓta​(f∘hm†​(Zi),Yi)\widehat{f}_{n,h_{m}^{\dagger}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(f\circ h_{m}^{\dagger}(Z_{i}),Y_{i})be the corresponding estimator of the target model.
Suppose that, for every deterministic sequencehm†∈ℋso,m​(0)h_{m}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0),‖f^n,hm†∘hm†−fn,hm†∙∘hm†‖Pta,2\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{n,h_{m}^{\dagger}}\circ h_{m}^{\dagger}-f_{n,h_{m}^{\dagger}}^{\bullet}\circ h_{m}^{\dagger}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=oPta​(1),\displaystyle=o_{P_{\operatorname{ta}}}(1),‖f^n,hm†∘hm†−ata∗‖Pta,2\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{n,h_{m}^{\dagger}}\circ h_{m}^{\dagger}-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=oPta​(1).\displaystyle=o_{P_{\operatorname{ta}}}(1).

Then,ℋso,m​(0)⊆ℋta,m​(ρta,n)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{ta},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})for some sequenceρta,n=o​(1)\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=o(1).

## Remark 4.

Theνn\nu_{n}-transferability condition inAssumption˜2is stronger than necessary for convergence of the target model for simplicity of presentation.
A weaker sufficient condition is thatminf∈ℱnsub⁡‖f∘h𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘−fn∘hm‖Pta,2=OP​(δso,m/νn)\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\left\|f\circ\widecheck{h}-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n})for every sequence of estimated embedding functionsh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘∈ℋm\widecheck{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}satisfying˜12.
Indeed, this weaker condition is necessary in the following sense: ifϵta,n=O​(δso,m/νn)\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}=O(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}),rta,n=O​(δso,m/νn)r_{\operatorname{ta},n}=O(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}), and‖f^∘h𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘−ata∗‖Pta,2=OP​(δso,m/νn)\left\|\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}-a^{*}_{\operatorname{ta}}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}\left(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}\right)for every sequence of estimated embedding functionsh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘∈ℋm\widecheck{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}satisfying˜12, thenminf∈ℱnsub⁡‖f∘h𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘−fn∘hm‖Pta,2=OP​(δso,m/νn)\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\left\|f\circ\widecheck{h}-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}). The proof is similar to the one forTheorem˜2.

## 3.3Closer Look at Task Diversity

In this subsection, we discuss sufficient and necessary conditions for task diversity inDefinition˜2.

## 3.3.1Sufficient Condition

We first assume that the density ratio ofZZin the source and target tasks is bounded away from zero.

## Assumption 3(Prediction Bound for Source Model).

There existsw¯n>0\underline{w}_{n}>0such thatpso​(z)/pta​(z)≥w¯n2p_{\operatorname{so}}(z)/p_{\operatorname{ta}}(z)\geq\underline{w}_{n}^{2},PtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-a.s., wherepso​(z)p_{\operatorname{so}}(z)andpta​(z)p_{\operatorname{ta}}(z)are the densities ofZZin the source and target samples with respect to some measures, respectively.

Assumption˜3holds only if the support ofZZin the target task is included in the support ofZZin the source task.
We alloww¯n\underline{w}_{n}to decrease slowly to zero asnnincreases, but not too quickly.
Although it is commonly assumed in the machine learning literature (e.g.,Johanssonet al.,2019), the density ratio condition is a strong assumption especially whenZZis unstructured data.
Relaxing this assumption is beyond the scope of this paper and is an important direction for future research.

## Theorem 3(Sufficient Condition for Transferability on the Mean Squared Loss).

Suppose that there exists a Lipschitz functionΓ:ℝT→ℝ\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{T}\to\mathbb{R}with Lipschitz constantLΓL_{\Gamma}such that‖fn∘hm−Γ∘gm∘hm‖Pta,2≤ϱn\|f_{n}\circ h_{m}-\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}\leq\varrho_{n}for some toleranceϱn≥0\varrho_{n}\geq 0.
For thisΓ\Gamma, we assume that for everyh∈ℋso,m​(ρso,m)h\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}), there existsf∈ℱnsubf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}such that‖f∘h−Γ∘gm∙∘h‖Pta,2≤ϱn\left\|f\circ h-\Gamma\circ g^{\bullet}_{m}\circ h\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}\leq\varrho_{n}, wheregm∙∈arg​ming∈𝒢m⁡‖g∘h−gm∘hm‖Pso,2g^{\bullet}_{m}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\left\|g\circ h-g_{m}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}.
Then, underAssumption˜3,gmg_{m}is(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}for everyρso,m≥0\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\geq 0andρta,n=LΓ​ρso,m/w¯n+2​ϱn\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=L_{\Gamma}\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}/\underline{w}_{n}+2\varrho_{n}.

This theorem provides a sufficient condition for(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diversity based on the existence of a Lipschitz functionΓ\Gammathat relates the target modelfn∘hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}to the source modelgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}.
The first condition requires that the target model can be approximated by applying the Lipschitz functionΓ\Gammato the source model with some small errorϱn\varrho_{n}.
The second condition requires that for every embedding functionhhclose to the source task identified set, there exists a target modelffthat can approximateΓ∘gm∙∘h\Gamma\circ g^{\bullet}_{m}\circ hwith some small errorϱn\varrho_{n}.
Under these conditions and the density ratio condition inAssumption˜3, the taskgmg_{m}is(ρso,m,LΓ​ρso,m/w¯n+2​ϱn)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},L_{\Gamma}\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}/\underline{w}_{n}+2\varrho_{n})-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}for everyρso,m≥0\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\geq 0.
Note that transferability is stronger if the Lipschitz constantLΓL_{\Gamma}is small, the approximation errorϱn\varrho_{n}is small, and the constantw¯n\underline{w}_{n}related to the infimum of the density ratio is large.

In the extreme caseLΓ=0L_{\Gamma}=0(i.e.,Γ\Gammais constant), we have(ρso,m,2​ϱn)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},2\varrho_{n})-diversity.
Also, ifLΓL_{\Gamma}diverges to infinity, the(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diversity condition does not hold for every finiteρta,n\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}.
In summary, a smallLΓL_{\Gamma}leads to better transferability.

In the next example, we verify the sufficient condition inTheorem˜3when both the computer scientist and the economist use linear models.

satisfiesfn∘hm=Γ∘gm∘hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}=\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}for any linearfnf_{n}, whereBso,m+:=(Bso,m′​Bso,m)−1​Bso,m′B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{+}:=(B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\prime}B_{\operatorname{so},m})^{-1}B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\prime}is the Moore-Penrose inverse ofBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}.
