belongs toℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}. In particular, the displayed linear model belongs toℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}ifℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}contains all linear models.

Example˜9shows that, when the target class contains all linear heads with intercepts, the row-span condition is sufficient for transferability withϱn=0\varrho_{n}=0. Under the regularity conditions inTheorem˜4, the same condition is also necessary for(0,0)(0,0)-diversity. Intuitively, if the coefficients of the source tasks are informative enough to represent the coefficients of the target task, the transferability condition holds.
Thus, if we want to guarantee transferability uniformly over all target tasks for every possibleβta\beta_{\operatorname{ta}}, this requires at leastKKsource tasks (T≥KT\geq K).
The next corollary states sufficient conditions for general Lipschitz target models, which include the linear target case as a special case.

## Corollary 1(Sufficient Condition for Transferability for Linear Source Models).

Let𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}be the class of linear source headsgα,B​(v)=α+B​vg_{\alpha,B}(v)=\alpha+Bvwithα∈ℝT\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{T}andB∈ℝT×KB\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}, and suppose thatgm∘hm=αso,m+Bso,m​hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}=\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}+B_{\operatorname{so},m}h_{m}, whereαso,m∈ℝT\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}andBso,m∈ℝT×KB_{\operatorname{so},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}.
Consider any Lipschitz target model classℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}with Lipschitz constantLℱL_{\mathcal{F}}. Suppose that, for everyρso,m≥0\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\geq 0, everyh∈ℋso,m​(ρso,m)h\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}), and every corresponding best linear source head(αso,m∙,Bso,m∙)∈arg​minα∈ℝT,B∈ℝT×K⁡‖α+B​h−(αso,m+Bso,m​hm)‖Pso,2,(\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\bullet},B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\bullet})\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{T},\,B\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}}\left\|\alpha+Bh-(\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}+B_{\operatorname{so},m}h_{m})\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2},

there existsf∈ℱnsubf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}such thatf∘h=fn​(Bso,m+​(αso,m∙−αso,m+Bso,m∙​h)),Pta​-a.s.f\circ h=f_{n}\left(B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{+}(\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\bullet}-\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}+B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\bullet}h)\right),\qquad P_{\operatorname{ta}}\text{-a.s.}

AssumeAssumption˜3holds. IfBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}has full column rank, thengmg_{m}is(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}for everyρso,m≥0\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\geq 0, whereρta,n=Lℱw¯n​σmin​(Bso,m)​ρso,m.\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=\frac{L_{\mathcal{F}}}{\underline{w}_{n}\sigma_{\min}(B_{\operatorname{so},m})}\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}.

The corollary implies that, when the source task is linear and the target task is Lipschitz, theνn\nu_{n}-transferability condition holds if the source task coefficients are informative enough to represent the target task coefficients.
Moreover, the transferability measureνn=w¯n​σmin​(Bso,m)/Lℱ\nu_{n}=\underline{w}_{n}\sigma_{\min}(B_{\operatorname{so},m})/L_{\mathcal{F}}is larger if the source task has a larger minimum singular valueσmin​(Bso,m)\sigma_{\min}(B_{\operatorname{so},m}), the target task has a smaller Lipschitz constantLℱL_{\mathcal{F}}, and the density ratio ofZZin the source and target tasks is bounded away from zero with a larger value ofw¯n\underline{w}_{n}.

## 3.3.2Necessary Condition

The following theorems provide necessary conditions for task diversity. We start with the linear case as inExample˜9.
WhenBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}is rank deficient, there is a directionv​(Z)v(Z)in the embedding space that is not captured by any of the source tasks, but it can be relevant for the target task.
Intuitively, if the source tasks are not diverse enough to cover all directions of the target task, transferability generally fails for the uncovered directions of the source task coefficients. See the next theorem for a formal statement.

## Theorem 4(Necessary Condition for Linear Models).

Suppose that the population source and target heads are linear as inExample˜9, so thatgm∘hm=αso,m+Bso,m​hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}=\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}+B_{\operatorname{so},m}h_{m}andfn∘hm=αta,n+βta,n′​hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}=\alpha_{\operatorname{ta},n}+\beta_{\operatorname{ta},n}^{\prime}h_{m}. Assume that𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}contains every linear source headv↦α+B​vv\mapsto\alpha+Bv, whereα∈ℝT\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{T}andB∈ℝT×KB\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}, and that everyf∈ℱnsubf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}is a linear target headv↦α+β′​vv\mapsto\alpha+\beta^{\prime}v, whereα∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}andβ∈ℝK\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{K}.
Assume also that𝔼ta​[(1,hm​(Z)′)′​(1,hm​(Z)′)]\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[(1,h_{m}(Z)^{\prime})^{\prime}(1,h_{m}(Z)^{\prime})]is positive definite and that, for every unit vectorv∈ℝKv\in\mathbb{R}^{K}, there exists somec∈ℝKc\in\mathbb{R}^{K}such that(IK−v​v′)​hm+c∈ℋm(I_{K}-vv^{\prime})h_{m}+c\in\mathcal{H}_{m}.
Ifgmg_{m}is(0,0)(0,0)-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m},
then there exists a vectorb∈ℝTb\in\mathbb{R}^{T}such thatβta,n′=b′​Bso,m\beta_{\operatorname{ta},n}^{\prime}=b^{\prime}B_{\operatorname{so},m}, and there exists an affine functionΓ​(x)=αta,n+βta,n′​Bso,m+​(x−αso,m)\Gamma(x)=\alpha_{\operatorname{ta},n}+\beta_{\operatorname{ta},n}^{\prime}B_{\operatorname{so},m}^{+}(x-\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m})such thatfn∘hm​(Z)=Γ∘gm∘hm​(Z)f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)=\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z),PtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-a.s.

The assumption(IK−v​v′)​hm+c∈ℋm(I_{K}-vv^{\prime})h_{m}+c\in\mathcal{H}_{m}is a technical condition that allows us to construct a functionhhthat attains the same source prediction ashmh_{m}but a different target prediction fromhmh_{m}.
For some functional classesℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}such as the class of ReLU activated hidden layer functions,ℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}only contains nonnegative functions, but it satisfies this assumption by choosingccto be sufficiently large ifℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}is sufficiently rich.

The following theorem provides necessary conditions for transferability in a general model with a nonlinear source task under sufficiently rich function classesℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}and𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}so that the nonlinear analog of the constructionhhin the proof ofTheorem˜4is possible.
Note that we do not put any restriction on the function classℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}.

## Theorem 5(Necessary Condition for Transferability in General Models).

Assume thatℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}and𝒢m,t\mathcal{G}_{m,t}fort=1,…,Tt=1,\ldots,Tare classes of square-integrable functions with respect toPtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}andPsoP_{\operatorname{so}}.
We also assume that there existsh0∈ℋso,m​(0)h_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)such thath0≠hmh_{0}\neq h_{m}.
Then, ifgmg_{m}is(0,0)(0,0)-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m},
there exists a measurable functionΓ0:ℝK→ℝ\Gamma_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}such thatfn∘hm​(Z)=Γ0∘h0​(Z)f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)=\Gamma_{0}\circ h_{0}(Z),PtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-a.s.

In particular, suppose thatσ​(h0​(Z))⊆σ​(gm∘hm​(Z))¯Pta,\sigma(h_{0}(Z))\subseteq\overline{\sigma(g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z))}^{P_{\operatorname{ta}}},

where the right-hand side is thePtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-completion defined inLemma˜3.
Then there exists a measurable functionΓ:ℝT→ℝ\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{T}\to\mathbb{R}such thatfn∘hm​(Z)=Γ∘gm∘hm​(Z)f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)=\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z),PtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-a.s.

The existence of a distincth0h_{0}inTheorem˜5means that the population source model does not uniquely identify the embedding. Such source nonidentification commonly arises in deep neural networks, where different hidden representations can be paired with compensating source heads. As(0,0)(0,0)-diversity requiresℋso,m​(0)⊆ℋta,m​(0)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{ta},m}(0), the existence ofΓ0\Gamma_{0}is needed to adapt the nonidentified embedding function.
The sigma-field inclusion condition is an additional information condition for
the target distribution. For the sameh0h_{0}, it states that, up toPtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-null sets, the population source outputgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}contains all the information onh0​(Z)h_{0}(Z). Sufficiently rich classes𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}andℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}are needed for the construction of a source-equivalenth0h_{0}satisfying this condition.
Under this additional condition, the existence ofΓ\Gammais necessary for(0,0)(0,0)-diversity.

Under the head-class and regularity conditions inTheorems˜4and5, the necessary conditions show that the corresponding sufficient condition is also necessary in these settings.
These theorems suggest that the sufficient condition inTheorem˜3withϱn=0\varrho_{n}=0, namelyfn∘hm​(Z)=Γ∘gm∘hm​(Z)f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)=\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z),PtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-a.s., is also necessary for the transferability condition in these cases.
However, the next example shows that the transferability condition may not hold even if bothfnf_{n}andgmg_{m}are linear but the pre-training model class𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}is nonlinear.

## Example 10(Counterexample for Nonlinear Pre-training Model).

Suppose thathm​(Z)∈ℝ+h_{m}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}and both tasks use the mean squared loss.
Suppose thatℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}is a set of linear models as inExample˜9, but𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}includes any shallow neural networks with one hidden layer with ReLU activation of width 2.
Suppose thatZ∼Unif​[−1,1]Z\sim\textrm{Unif}\ [-1,1],hm​(Z)=|Z|h_{m}(Z)=|Z|,fn​(⋅)=gm​(⋅)=(⋅)f_{n}(\cdot)=g_{m}(\cdot)=(\cdot)are identity maps andT=1T=1.
If we chooseh​(Z)=Z+1h(Z)=Z+1, then we haveming∈𝒢m⁡‖g∘h​(Z)−gm∘hm​(Z)‖Pso,22=0,\min_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\left\|g\circ h(Z)-g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z)\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}^{2}=0,

sinceg∈𝒢mg\in\mathcal{G}_{m}can approximate the functiongm∘hm​(Z)=|Z|g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z)=|Z|, for example, byg​(x)=ReLU⁡(x−1)+ReLU⁡(−(x−1))g(x)=\operatorname{ReLU}(x-1)+\operatorname{ReLU}(-(x-1)).
On the other hand,minf∈ℱnsub⁡‖f∘h−fn∘hm‖P,22=𝔼​[(1/2−|Z|)2]=1/12>0,\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\left\|f\circ h-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P,2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[(1/2-|Z|)^{2}]=1/12>0,

sincef∈ℱnsubf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}is linear and cannot approximatefn∘hm​(Z)=|Z|f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)=|Z|perfectly.
Thus,gmg_{m}is not(0,0)(0,0)-diverse overfnf_{n}with respect tohmh_{m}.

From this section, we have seen that the transferability condition is substantially stronger for nonlinear source heads𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}than for linear source heads𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}.
This is because the nonlinear source head𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}can approximate a much richer class of functions than the linear source head𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}, which makes it more difficult to satisfy the diversity condition.
Therefore, if the economist is indifferent between linear and nonlinear source heads, the linear source head is preferable for guaranteeing the transferability condition.

## 4Convergence Rate of Transfer Learning under Lower-Level Conditions

## 4.1Low-dimensional Case

In this section, we provide lower-level primitive conditions for the convergence rates for the transfer-learning problem without the high-level conditions ofAssumption˜1.
We first introduce some notation.
For a function classℱ\mathcal{F}, let𝖵​(ℱ)\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F})be the VC dimension of the set of subgraphs of functions inℱ\mathcal{F}.191919Thesubgraphof a functionf:𝒳→ℝf:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}is the subset of𝒳×ℝ\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}given by{(x,y):y<f​(x)}\{(x,y):y<f(x)\}. The VC dimension of a set of subgraphs is defined as the largest integer such that there exists a set of points with cardinality equal to the integer that can be shattered by the subgraphs. Seevan der Vaart and Wellner (2023), Section 2.6 for more details.To avoid technical complications concerning the measurability of the supremum, we assume that the functional classesℱn\mathcal{F}_{n},ℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}, and𝒢m∘ℋm\mathcal{G}_{m}\circ\mathcal{H}_{m}are pointwise measurable.202020A measurable functional classℱ\mathcal{F}ispointwise measurableif it contains a countable subsetℱ0\mathcal{F}^{0}such that for everyf∈ℱf\in\mathcal{F}there exists a sequencefj∈ℱ0f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}withfj​(v)→f​(v)f_{j}(v)\rightarrow f(v)for everyvv, where the convergence is with respect to the Euclidean norm. Seevan der Vaart and Wellner (2023), Section 2.3 for more details.

We start with the convergence rate of the target model given the estimated embedding functionhwidecheck\widecheck{h}from the source task.
Before we state the theorem on the convergence rate of the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}, we assume one more condition on the target loss function.

## Assumption 4(Conditions on Target Task).
- 1.

For every sequence{ρso,m}m=1∞\{\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}such thatρso,m=O​(δso,m)\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}=O(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}), everyf∈ℱnf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}, and everyh∈ℋso,m​(ρso,m)h\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}), we have‖f∘h−ata∗‖Pta,22≲𝔼ta​[ℓta​(f∘h​(Z),Y)]−𝔼ta​[ℓta​(ata∗​(Z),Y)]≲‖f∘h−ata∗‖Pta,22\left\|f\circ h-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}^{2}\lesssim\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(f\circ h(Z),Y)]-\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}(Z),Y)]\lesssim\left\|f\circ h-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}^{2}

for large enoughmmandnn.
- 2.

The loss functionℓta​(⋅,⋅)\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(\cdot,\cdot)is Lipschitz continuous in the first argument with some constantCℓ,ta>0C_{\ell,\operatorname{ta}}>0.

This assumption requires that the excess risk of the target loss function is bounded above and below by the squaredL2L^{2}-norm.
This assumption is standard in statistical learning theory and is imposed in many existing works (e.g.,Farrellet al.,2021).Lemma˜4verifies the excess-risk comparison and the required Lipschitz comparison over uniformly bounded candidate-score ranges for least squares and binary logistic loss.

## Theorem 6(Convergence Rate of Target Model).

Letrta,n=𝖵​(ℱn)​log⁡(n)n.r_{\operatorname{ta},n}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F}_{n})\log(n)}{n}}.(15)

For every estimated embedding functionh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widecheck{h}satisfyingAssumption˜1(i), letf^∈ℱn\widehat{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}be an estimated target model satisfying1n​∑i=1nℓta​(f^∘hwidecheck​(Zi),Yi)\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i}),Y_{i})≤minf∈ℱn⁡1n​∑i=1nℓta​(f∘hwidecheck​(Zi),Yi)\displaystyle\leq\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{\operatorname{ta}}(f\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i}),Y_{i})+OP​(rta,n2+(δso,m/νn)2+ϵta,n2).\displaystyle\quad+O_{P}(r_{\operatorname{ta},n}^{2}+(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n})^{2}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}^{2}).(16)

Suppose that‖f‖∞≤MF<∞\|f\|_{\infty}\leq M_{F}<\inftyfor everyf∈ℱnf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}.
Then, underAssumption˜1(i),Assumptions˜2,3and4, we have‖f^∘hwidecheck−ata∗‖Pta,2=OP​(rta,n+δso,m/νn+ϵta,n).\left\|\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}\left(r_{\operatorname{ta},n}+\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\nu_{n}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}\right).(17)

The theorem shows that, under standard conditions on the function classℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}and the target loss function, the convergence rate of the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}to the true modelata∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}is governed by the VC dimension ofℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}and the target sample sizenn.

## Remark 5(Fine-tuning).

Our theory accommodates fine-tuning by allowingh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widecheck{h}to differ from the source task estimator. This remains valid providedh𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widecheck{h}is estimated on a sample independent of the final parameter estimation sample andf^∘h𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}converges tofn∘hmf_{n}\circ h_{m}faster thann−1/4n^{-1/4}.
Thus, the economist can fine-tune the embedding function using the same sample to trainf^\widehat{f}, provided fine-tuning improves the target model convergence rate relative to the unfine-tuned embedding function, whose convergence rate is given byTheorem˜6.
Since fine-tuning does not necessarily improve the accuracy of the target model (Kumaret al.,2022, Table 1), it is important to check the convergence rate of the fine-tuned embedding function to ensure that the fine-tuning is not harmful to the convergence rate of the target model.
For example, the economist can check by cross-validation whether the loss off^∘h𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}with fine-tuning is smaller than the loss without fine-tuning. The formal hypothesis testing procedure for this comparison is proposed byFava (2025).

Next, we provide a theorem on the convergence rate of the source modelgwidecheck∘hwidecheck\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}to the population source modelgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}.
The rate of convergence for deep learning models is an active area of research and can be improved in future work. Importantly, our transfer learning rate of the target task uses the convergence rate of the source model as an input; thus, any improvement in the convergence rate of the source model can be directly translated into an improved convergence rate of the target model by our theory.

## Assumption 5(Conditions on Source Task).
- 1.

For everyg∈𝒢mg\in\mathcal{G}_{m}andh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m},τm2​‖g∘h−aso∗‖Pso,22≲𝔼so​[ℓso​(g∘h​(Z),S)]−𝔼so​[ℓso​(aso∗​(Z),S)]≲‖g∘h−aso∗‖Pso,22\tau_{m}^{2}\left\|g\circ h-a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}^{2}\lesssim\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{so}}[\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(g\circ h(Z),S)]-\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{so}}[\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}(Z),S)]\lesssim\left\|g\circ h-a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}^{2}(18)

with some sequenceτm∈(0,1]\tau_{m}\in(0,1]for large enoughmmandnn.

There exists a sequenceL𝒢,m≥1L_{\mathcal{G},m}\geq 1such that everyg∈𝒢mg\in\mathcal{G}_{m}isL𝒢,mL_{\mathcal{G},m}-Lipschitz with respect to the Euclidean norm, uniformly over𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}. That is, for everyv,v~∈𝒱v,\widetilde{v}\in\mathcal{V},‖g​(v)−g​(v~)‖2≤L𝒢,m​‖v−v~‖2.\|g(v)-g(\widetilde{v})\|_{2}\leq L_{\mathcal{G},m}\|v-\widetilde{v}\|_{2}.

The loss conditions in parts (i)-(ii) are satisfied withτm=T−1\tau_{m}=T^{-1}if the loss function is the multinomial logistic loss and the Euclidean-norm boundMMinLemma˜5holds uniformly inmmandTTover allg∈𝒢mg\in\mathcal{G}_{m}andh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m}and foraso∗a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}.
Part (iii) is a regularity condition on the source-head class rather than the loss function. It rules out discontinuous heads and allows the entropy of the composite class𝒢m∘ℋm\mathcal{G}_{m}\circ\mathcal{H}_{m}to be controlled by the coordinatewise entropies of𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}andℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}.

The next theorem relates the convergence rate of the source model inAssumption˜1(i) to the VC dimensions of the function classesℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}and𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}.

## Theorem 7(Convergence Rate of Source Model).

Letrso,m=∑k=1K𝖵​(ℋm,k)​log⁡(m​L𝒢,m​K)+∑t=1T𝖵​(𝒢m,t)​log⁡(m​T)τm2​m.r_{\operatorname{so},m}=\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{H}_{m,k})\log\left(mL_{\mathcal{G},m}K\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{G}_{m,t})\log\left(mT\right)}{\tau_{m}^{2}m}}.(19)

Leth𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘∈ℋm\widecheck{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}andg𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘∈𝒢m\widecheck{g}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}be any estimated embedding function and source model estimator satisfying1m​∑i=1mℓso​(gwidecheck∘hwidecheck​(Zi),Si)\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z_{i}),S_{i})≤ming∈𝒢m,h∈ℋm⁡1m​∑i=1mℓso​(g∘h​(Zi),Si)\displaystyle\leq\min_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m},h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(g\circ h(Z_{i}),S_{i})+OPso​(rso,m2+ϵso,m2),\displaystyle\quad+O_{P_{\operatorname{so}}}(r_{\operatorname{so},m}^{2}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}^{2}),(20)

Then, underAssumption˜5, we have‖gwidecheck∘hwidecheck−gm∘hm‖Pso,2\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}-g_{m}\circ h_{m}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}=OPso​(rso,m/τm+ϵso,m/τm),\displaystyle=O_{P_{\operatorname{so}}}\left(r_{\operatorname{so},m}/\tau_{m}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}/\tau_{m}\right),(21)‖gwidecheck∘hwidecheck−aso∗‖Pso,2\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}-a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}=OPso​(rso,m/τm+ϵso,m/τm).\displaystyle=O_{P_{\operatorname{so}}}\left(r_{\operatorname{so},m}/\tau_{m}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}/\tau_{m}\right).(22)

This theorem states that under some standard conditions on the function classesℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}and𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}and the source loss function, the convergence rate of the source modelgwidecheck∘hwidecheck\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}to the population modelgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}(or the true modelaso∗a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}) is determined by the coordinatewise VC dimensions ofℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}and𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}, the logarithmic factors induced by the embedding dimensionKK, the output dimensionTT, and the head Lipschitz constantL𝒢,mL_{\mathcal{G},m}, the source sample sizemm, and the curvature termτm\tau_{m}of the source loss function.

Theorems˜6and7together imply that, under the transferability assumption, the convergence rate of the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}to the true modelata∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}is governed mainly by five components: the ratio of the VC dimension of the target model classℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}to the target sample sizenn; the ratio of the source complexities forℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}and𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}to the source sample sizemm; the transferability rateνn\nu_{n}; the approximation error termsϵta,n\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}andϵso,m\epsilon_{\operatorname{so},m}; and the curvature termτm\tau_{m}of the source loss function.
For example,𝖵​(ℱn)=O​(K)\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=O(K)ifℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}is a class of linear models withKKregressors, and𝖵​(ℱn)=O​(Wn​Ln​log⁡(Wn))\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=O(W_{n}L_{n}\log(W_{n}))ifℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}is a class of DNNs with widthWnW_{n}and depthLnL_{n}(Bartlettet al.,2019, Theorem 7).
In particular, the rate improves when both function class complexities are small relative to sample size and the transferability and approximation error terms are negligible.
If the complexity of the function classes is smaller than the square root of the sample sizes, and the transferability and approximation error terms are sufficiently small, the target model can achieve a convergence rate faster thann−1/4n^{-1/4}.

The derived rate is faster than the one inTripuraneniet al.(2020)since we effectively use the curvature condition and the boundedness of the loss function.
Also, our rate is comparable to the result inWatkinset al.(2023)although their fast rate assumes𝔼so​[ℓso​(aso∗​(Z),S)]=0\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{so}}[\ell_{\operatorname{so}}(a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}(Z),S)]=0, which is a strong and unrealistic assumption in practice.
Without this assumption, their rate becomes slower than ours and comparable to the one inTripuraneniet al.(2020).
Importantly, the slow rate inTripuraneniet al.(2020)is not enough to apply the DML framework since it is always slower thann−1/4n^{-1/4}.

We can directly apply the above theorems to derive the convergence rates of transfer learning when the target sample sizennis large enough compared to its input dimensionKK.
This strategy is taken in, for example,Bajariet al.(2025)andBachet al.(2024).
Since the VC dimension is well studied for many specific models such as linear models and DNNs (Bartlettet al.,2019), the above theorems can be applied to derive the convergence rates of transfer learning for these specific models under some conditions on the transferability and approximation errors.
For DNNs, the approximation error can be small if the true model is sufficiently smooth and the DNN has enough width and depth (Yarotsky,2017).

## 4.2High-dimensional Case

## 4.2.1Convergence ofhhfor linear source headsgg

For the low-dimensional case, the VC dimension of the function classℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}is small, andTheorem˜6is directly applicable to derive the convergence rate of the target modelf^∘hwidecheck\widehat{f}\circ\widecheck{h}to the true modelata∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}.
An important feature of the VC dimension is that it is a pure functional complexity measure and does not depend on the distribution of the input data.
On the other hand,Theorem˜6is not directly applicable to the high-dimensional case since the VC dimension ofℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}is large, and we need to assume some additional structure on the input data, i.e., embeddings, to derive the convergence rate of the target model.

In this section, we focus on the case in which the source model has a linear head as inExample˜9andhm​(Z)∈ℝKh_{m}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}is the last hidden layer of the source model.
In this case, we have a convergence rate of the estimated embedding functionhwidecheck\widecheck{h}to an approximately true embedding functionhmh_{m}up to a linear transformation.
Recall that the population source model isgm∘hm​(Z)=αso,m+Bso,m​hm​(Z)g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z)=\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}+B_{\operatorname{so},m}h_{m}(Z)for some vectorαso,m∈ℝT\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}, some matrixBso,m∈ℝT×KB_{\operatorname{so},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}, and an embedding functionhm​(Z)∈ℝKh_{m}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}.
The pre-trained source model isgwidecheck∘hwidecheck​(Z)=αwidecheckso+Bwidecheckso​hwidecheck​(Z)\widecheck{g}\circ\widecheck{h}(Z)=\widecheck{\alpha}_{\operatorname{so}}+\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}\widecheck{h}(Z)for some vectorαwidecheckso∈ℝT\widecheck{\alpha}_{\operatorname{so}}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}, some matrixBwidecheckso∈ℝT×K\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}, and an embedding functionhwidecheck​(Z)∈ℝK\widecheck{h}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}estimated from the source task.
Define a transformed embedding functionhwidecheckproj\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}}as the projection ofhwidecheck\widecheck{h}onto the row space ofBwidecheckso\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}as follows:hwidecheckproj:=Bwidecheckso+​Bwidecheckso​hwidecheck\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}}:=\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}^{+}\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}\widecheck{h}(23)

The following lemma gives anL2L_{2}convergence rate for the projected embeddinghwidecheckproj\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}}toward an affine transformationqwidecheck+Qwidecheck​hm\widecheck{q}+\widecheck{Q}h_{m}ofhmh_{m}, whereqwidecheck:=Bwidecheckso+​(αso,m−αwidecheckso)\widecheck{q}:=\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}^{+}(\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m}-\widecheck{\alpha}_{\operatorname{so}})is the shift term andQwidecheck:=Bwidecheckso+​Bso,m\widecheck{Q}:=\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}}^{+}B_{\operatorname{so},m}is the linear transformation ofhmh_{m}.

## 4.2.2LASSO Target Model

We do not recommend using LASSO for the target model for the following reason.
As we have seen inSection˜4.2.1,ℋso,m​(0)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)includes invertible linear transformations of an approximately true embedding functionhmh_{m}.Theorem˜2suggests that, to ensure‖f^n,hm†∘hm†−ata∗‖Pta,2=oPta​(1)\|\widehat{f}_{n,h_{m}^{\dagger}}\circ h_{m}^{\dagger}-a^{*}_{\operatorname{ta}}\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=o_{P_{\operatorname{ta}}}(1)for any sequencehm†∈ℋso,m​(0)h_{m}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0), it is necessary thatℋso,m​(0)⊆ℋta,m​(ρta,n)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{ta},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})for some sequenceρta,n=o​(1)\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=o(1)when the target model has small estimation and approximation errors.
That is, for every invertible matrixQ∈ℝK×KQ\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}and every vectorq∈ℝKq\in\mathbb{R}^{K}such thatq+Q​hm∈ℋmq+Qh_{m}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}, there must exist somef∈ℱnsubf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}such that‖fn∘hm−f∘(q+Q​hm)‖Pta,2\|f_{n}\circ h_{m}-f\circ(q+Qh_{m})\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}converges to zero asn→∞n\rightarrow\infty.
Note that linear regression, ridge regression, and DNNs satisfy this condition since they transform the input vector by a linear transformation.

Therefore, our theory recommends avoiding methods that rely on assumptions, such as sparsity, that are not preserved under the unidentified invertible linear transformations of the embedding functionhmh_{m}(e.g.,Wainwright,2019, Chapter 7).
Suppose the economist uses LASSO for the target model given the estimated embedding functionhwidecheck\widecheck{h}from the source task.
To use LASSO, the existing theory typically requires an approximate-sparsity assumption.
On the other hand, sinceℋso,m​(0)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0)includes invertible linear transformations of an approximately true embedding functionhmh_{m}, Theorem 1 inKolesáret al.(2026)implies that the approximate sparsity assumption is violated in general.
Thus, even if the economist can justify the approximate sparsity assumption for an embedding functionhmh_{m}, the approximate sparsity assumption is hard to justify for otherh∈ℋso,m​(0)h\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0).
Since we cannot identify the embedding functionhmh_{m}from the source task, any method relying on variable selection can be fragile.

## 4.2.3Ridge Target Model

Section˜4.2.2shows that approximate sparsity is not preserved under the linear reparameterizations left unidentified by the source task. We therefore study anℓ2\ell_{2}-penalized linear target model as a tractable alternative.

The scale of a linear source head is not identified separately from the scale of its embedding. Thus, the condition in˜24is not invariant to observationally equivalent rescalings. Multiplying the embedding by a large constant and dividing the source-head matrix by the same constant can make its smallest positive singular value arbitrarily small.
This scale indeterminacy is not a problem for the low-dimensional case since the VC dimension is invariant to scaling. However, the scale indeterminacy is typically a problem for the high-dimensional case since we use the structure on the embeddings and the source head to derive the convergence rate of the target model.
The ridge theory below therefore imposes fixed singular value bounds on the source-head matrices used in the analysis.

Fix constantsCα,so<∞C_{\alpha,\operatorname{so}}<\inftyand0<σ¯≤σ¯<∞0<\underline{\sigma}\leq\overline{\sigma}<\infty, and letΘso,m\Theta_{\operatorname{so},m}be a deterministic, nonempty, compact subset of{(α,B)∈ℝT×ℝT×K:‖α‖2≤Cα,so,rank⁡(B)≥1,‖B‖sp≤σ¯,σmin+​(B)≥σ¯}.\left\{(\alpha,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}:\|\alpha\|_{2}\leq C_{\alpha,\operatorname{so}},\operatorname{rank}(B)\geq 1,\|B\|_{\mathrm{sp}}\leq\overline{\sigma},\sigma_{\min}^{+}(B)\geq\underline{\sigma}\right\}.

We assume that the population source-head parameters(αso,m,Bso,m)(\alpha_{\operatorname{so},m},B_{\operatorname{so},m})and the pre-trained source-head parameters(αwidecheckso,Bwidecheckso)(\widecheck{\alpha}_{\operatorname{so}},\widecheck{B}_{\operatorname{so}})belong toΘso,m\Theta_{\operatorname{so},m}with probability approaching one.
The economist can always obtain them through a transformation inAppendix˜Dif they want to ensure this condition explicitly.

For each(α,B)∈Θso,m(\alpha,B)\in\Theta_{\operatorname{so},m}, definegα,B:v↦α+B​v.g_{\alpha,B}:v\mapsto\alpha+Bv.(26)

For this subsection, let𝒢m:={gα,B:(α,B)∈ℝT×ℝT×K}\mathcal{G}_{m}:=\{g_{\alpha,B}:(\alpha,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}\}be the class of linear source heads, so that{gα,B:(α,B)∈Θso,m}⊆𝒢m\{g_{\alpha,B}:(\alpha,B)\in\Theta_{\operatorname{so},m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{m}.
Thus,𝒢m\mathcal{G}_{m}may also contain linear heads with parameters outsideΘso,m\Theta_{\operatorname{so},m}. For every(α,B)∈Θso,m(\alpha,B)\in\Theta_{\operatorname{so},m},Lemma˜18gives‖B+‖sp≤σ¯−1\|B^{+}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}\leq\underline{\sigma}^{-1}.

For everyρ≥0\rho\geq 0, letℋso,mproj​(ρ):={h∈ℋmproj:ming∈𝒢m⁡‖g∘h−gm∘hm‖Pso,2≤ρ}\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\mathrm{proj}}(\rho):=\{h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\mathrm{proj}}:\min_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\|g\circ h-g_{m}\circ h_{m}\|_{P_{\operatorname{so}},2}\leq\rho\}.
Fix constantsCα,Cβ<∞C_{\alpha},C_{\beta}<\infty, and letΘn:={(α,β)∈ℝ×ℝK:|α|≤Cα,‖β‖2≤Cβ}.\Theta_{n}:=\left\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}:|\alpha|\leq C_{\alpha},\|\beta\|_{2}\leq C_{\beta}\right\}.

## Theorem 8(Convergence Rate of Ridge Target Model).

Suppose thatAssumption˜1(i) andAssumptions˜3and6hold. We also assumeAssumption˜4holds withℋso,m​(ρso,m)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m})replaced byℋso,mproj​(ρso,m)\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}^{\mathrm{proj}}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}). Letλn>0\lambda_{n}>0be deterministic and letf^nridge∘h𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘proj\widehat{f}_{n}^{\mathrm{ridge}}\circ\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}}be defined in˜28. Then,‖f^nridge∘hwidecheckproj−ata∗‖Pta,2\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{f}_{n}^{\mathrm{ridge}}\circ\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}}-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=\displaystyle=OP​(1+𝒩hm,n​(λn)n+λn+δso,m/w¯n+(δso,m/w¯n)1/2tr(Σhm,n)1/4n​λn+δso,mw¯n+ϵta,n).\displaystyle O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1+\mathcal{N}_{h_{m},n}(\lambda_{n})}{n}}+\lambda_{n}+\frac{\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\underline{w}_{n}+(\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\underline{w}_{n})^{1/2}\operatorname{tr}(\Sigma_{h_{m},n})^{1/4}}{\sqrt{n}\lambda_{n}}+\frac{\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}}{\underline{w}_{n}}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}\right).(30)

The first two terms in the above rate are the estimation errors of ridge regression, and the last two terms are the transferability and approximation errors.
The middle term arises from the difference between𝒩hwidecheckproj,n​(λn)\mathcal{N}_{\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}},n}(\lambda_{n})and𝒩hm,n​(λn)\mathcal{N}_{h_{m},n}(\lambda_{n}).

For a concrete rate, suppose that, uniformly overnnandj=1,…,Kj=1,\ldots,K,μj​(Σhm,n)≤Cμ​j−2​afor constants​Cμ​<∞​and​a>​1/2,\mu_{j}(\Sigma_{h_{m},n})\leq C_{\mu}j^{-2a}\qquad\text{for constants }C_{\mu}<\infty\text{ and }a>1/2,

andδso,m/w¯n+ϵta,n=O​(n−a/(2​a+1))\delta_{\operatorname{so},m}/\underline{w}_{n}+\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}=O(n^{-a/(2a+1)}). Then𝒩hm,n​(λ)≲λ−1/a\mathcal{N}_{h_{m},n}(\lambda)\lesssim\lambda^{-1/a}. Choosingλn≍n−a/(2​a+1)\lambda_{n}\asymp n^{-a/(2a+1)}gives‖f^nridge∘hwidecheckproj−ata∗‖Pta,2=OP​(max⁡{n−a/(2​a+1),n−(a+1)/(2​(2​a+1))}).\left\|\widehat{f}_{n}^{\mathrm{ridge}}\circ\widecheck{h}^{\mathrm{proj}}-a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}\right\|_{P_{\operatorname{ta}},2}=O_{P}\left(\max\left\{n^{-a/(2a+1)},n^{-(a+1)/(2(2a+1))}\right\}\right).

Ifa>1/2a>1/2, the above rate isoP​(n−1/4)o_{P}(n^{-1/4}), which is sufficient for the DML framework.
Thus, ridge is a recommended regularized alternative when the target model is linear and the embedding is nearly identified up to a well-conditioned linear distortion.

In practice, the tuning parameterλn\lambda_{n}for ridge is typically selected byKK-fold cross-validation that directly targets prediction risk; see, for example,Hastieet al.(2022)for a recent detailed discussion of cross-validation for ridge regression, including in over-parameterized settings.

A natural question is whether a similar convergence rate can be achieved by neural network models with regularization such as random dropout orℓ2\ell_{2}regularization.232323Another line of research in deep learning theory on convergence rates makes use of functional shape assumptions on the function of interest (Kohler and Langer,2021; Bhattacharyaet al.,2024) or data structure assumptions such as approximate manifold structure (Jiaoet al.,2023; Schulteet al.,2025). However, their theory relies on the VC-dimension bound derived byBartlettet al.(2019), which is always larger than the dimension of the raw input data. Thus, the convergence rate is not informative unless the dimension of the raw input data is treated as fixed relative to the sample size.We conjecture that the answer is positive.
For linear regression, dropout can be interpreted as a form of ridge regularization (Srivastavaet al.,2014, Section 9.1).Weiet al.(2020)demonstrate both theoretically and empirically that dropout can work as regularization for deep neural networks.
However, even whenhmh_{m}is directly observed, the convergence rate of the regularized neural network estimator is not well understood in the literature at the level of generality ofFarrellet al.(2021), to the best of our knowledge. Establishing the convergence rate of regularized neural network estimators for the target model in our setting is an important direction for future research.

Supplement to “Econometrics with Pre-trained Embeddings for Unstructured Data”

Yuya Shimizu

University of Wisconsin-Madison

## Contents

This supplementary appendix contains proofs of the results in the main text as well as auxiliary results.

## Additional Notation

In the supplementary appendix, we use the following notation in addition to the notation introduced in the main text.
For deterministic sequencesana_{n}andbnb_{n}and for a scalarε\varepsilon, we writean≲εbna_{n}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}b_{n}if there exists a constantCε>0C_{\varepsilon}>0, depending only onε\varepsilon, such thatan≤Cε​bna_{n}\leq C_{\varepsilon}b_{n}for largenn.
In the proofs,Cε,1,Cε,2,…C_{\varepsilon,1},C_{\varepsilon,2},\dotsdenote positive constants that depend only onε\varepsilon, whileC1,C2,…C_{1},C_{2},\dotsdenote positive absolute constants. These constants may denote different values in different proof sections.P∗P^{*}and𝔼∗\mathbb{E}^{*}denote the outer probability and outer expectation, respectively.
For two symmetric matricesA∈ℝK×KA\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}andA~∈ℝK×K\widetilde{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}, we writeA⪯A~A\preceq\widetilde{A}ifA~−A\widetilde{A}-Ais positive semi-definite.

## Appendix ARelated Results in the Literature

## A.1Transfer Learning in the Machine Learning Literature

While a version of the diversity condition inDefinition˜2is considered in the machine learning literature on the excess-risk scale (e.g.,Tripuraneniet al.,2020;Xu and Tewari,2021), we characterize the diversity condition by a novel relationship between the diversity condition and the set inclusion condition on the near-identified sets of the embedding function.
Although the machine learning literature provides some sufficient conditions for task diversity and rate bounds for transfer learning, this paper provides results that are more general and tailored to economic applications in the following three aspects.

First, our sufficient condition inTheorem˜3is more flexible than the existing results. The existing results focus on the full-rank coefficient matrixBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}for the linear source head, but our sufficient condition does not require the full-rank condition onBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}and allows for rank deficiency ofBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}as long as the row span ofBso,mB_{\operatorname{so},m}includes the target coefficientβta,n\beta_{\operatorname{ta},n}.
In addition, our sufficient condition inTheorem˜3is more flexible than the existing results as it allows different source and target covariate distributions and more general relationships between source and target models, including Lipschitz transformations.

Second, the necessity results inTheorems˜4and5are new to the best of our knowledge. The existing literature does not provide necessity results for the diversity condition, and our theorems show, under their respective head-class and regularity conditions, that our sufficient condition is also necessary for the linear head case and the rich source head case, respectively.

Finally, our rate of convergence of the target model is faster than the existing rates in the literature.
Importantly, existing rates in the literature are not fast enough to guarantee theoP​(n−1/4)o_{P}(n^{-1/4})rate required for the DML framework without the realizability assumption, which requires the risks for both the source and target tasks to converge to zero.
The realizability assumption is too strong for many economic applications since it requires a very small error variance for nonparametric regressions.
For example, Theorem 3 ofTripuraneniet al.(2020)shows that the excess risk of the target model is bounded by a rate always slower thanOP​(1/n)O_{P}(1/\sqrt{n}), which implies a rate slower thanOP​(n−1/4)O_{P}(n^{-1/4})for the target model itself under the convex loss assumption inAssumption˜4(i).
Theorems 1 and 2 ofWatkinset al.(2023)derive excess risk bounds for the target model under the realizability and non-realizability assumptions, respectively, but the rates are not fast enough to guarantee theoP​(n−1/4)o_{P}(n^{-1/4})rate for the target model without the realizability assumption.
On the other hand, our target model convergence rate matches the fast rate ofWatkinset al.(2023)under realizability, but does not require realizability.

## A.2Relations toEscanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026)

Escanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026)study locally robust estimation with generated regressors. While their theory suggests that the generated regressors affect inference on the parameter of interest in general, this effect of generated regressors does not arise in our setup.
Return to the setup inSection˜2.1, where we have a target parameterθ∗\theta^{*}and nuisance functionsη∗=(γ∗,α∗)\eta^{*}=(\gamma^{*},\alpha^{*}). The goal is to estimateθ∗\theta^{*}using the estimated nuisance functionsη^=(f^γ,f^α)∘hwidecheck\widehat{\eta}=(\widehat{f}_{\gamma},\widehat{f}_{\alpha})\circ\widecheck{h}while accounting for the estimation error in the embeddings.

To clarify the relationship, we first consider the case with no approximation error in the target taskϵta,n=0\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}=0, and we next consider the case with approximation error.
For exposition, we also focus on a partially linear model (Example˜1), but the same logic works for the other applications with closed-form Neyman orthogonal scores and nuisance functions taking conditional expectation forms, includingExamples˜2,3and4.

With no approximation errorϵta,n=0\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}=0, the population nuisance function in the target task isη∗=fη,n∘hm\eta^{*}=f_{\eta,n}\circ h_{m}.
The partially linear model inExample˜1hasη∗​(Zi)=(γ∗​(Zi),α∗​(Zi))=(𝔼ta​[Yi|Zi],𝔼ta​[Xi|Zi])\eta^{*}(Z_{i})=(\gamma^{*}(Z_{i}),\alpha^{*}(Z_{i}))=(\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y_{i}|Z_{i}],\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[X_{i}|Z_{i}])andfη,n∘hm​(Zi)=(fγ,n∘hm​(Zi),fα,n∘hm​(Zi))=(𝔼ta​[Yi|hm​(Zi)],𝔼ta​[Xi|hm​(Zi)])f_{\eta,n}\circ h_{m}(Z_{i})=(f_{\gamma,n}\circ h_{m}(Z_{i}),f_{\alpha,n}\circ h_{m}(Z_{i}))=(\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y_{i}|h_{m}(Z_{i})],\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[X_{i}|h_{m}(Z_{i})]).
Thus, our setup considers the case where𝔼ta​[Yi|Zi]=𝔼ta​[Yi|hm​(Zi)]\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y_{i}|Z_{i}]=\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y_{i}|h_{m}(Z_{i})]and𝔼ta​[Xi|Zi]=𝔼ta​[Xi|hm​(Zi)]\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[X_{i}|Z_{i}]=\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[X_{i}|h_{m}(Z_{i})].
On the other hand,Escanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026)consider a more general setup allowing𝔼ta​[Yi|Zi]≠𝔼ta​[Yi|hm​(Zi)]=fγ,n∘hm​(Zi)\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y_{i}|Z_{i}]\neq\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[Y_{i}|h_{m}(Z_{i})]=f_{\gamma,n}\circ h_{m}(Z_{i})and𝔼ta​[Xi|Zi]≠𝔼ta​[Xi|hm​(Zi)]=fα,n∘hm​(Zi)\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[X_{i}|Z_{i}]\neq\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[X_{i}|h_{m}(Z_{i})]=f_{\alpha,n}\circ h_{m}(Z_{i}).
In our setting,hm​(Zi)h_{m}(Z_{i})captures sufficient statistics ofZiZ_{i}for the target task, making the Neyman-orthogonal score insensitive to small perturbations in bothfηf_{\eta}andhh.
In the more general setting considered byEscanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026), the same Neyman-orthogonal score can be sensitive to perturbations in generated regressorsh​(Zi)h(Z_{i}).

More concretely, recall that the Neyman orthogonality condition is defined as follows:

## Definition 4(Neyman Orthogonality).

A moment functionψ​(W;θ,η)\psi(W;\theta,\eta)isNeyman orthogonalat(θ∗,η∗)(\theta^{*},\eta^{*})if∂∂r​𝔼ta​[ψ​(W;θ∗,η∗+r​(η−η∗))]|r=0=0\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\psi(W;\theta^{*},\eta^{*}+r(\eta-\eta^{*}))]\right|_{r=0}=0

for allη\etain some neighborhood ofη∗\eta^{*}.

Consider a Neyman-orthogonal score for the partially linear model:ψ​(Wi;θ,γ,α)={(Yi−γ​(Zi))−(Xi−α​(Zi))​θ}​(Xi−α​(Zi))\psi(W_{i};\theta,\gamma,\alpha)=\{(Y_{i}-\gamma(Z_{i}))-(X_{i}-\alpha(Z_{i}))\theta\}(X_{i}-\alpha(Z_{i})).
Under the regularity conditions for differentiability,∂∂r​𝔼ta​[ψ​(W;θ∗,fη,n∘hm+r​(fη∘h−fη,n∘hm))]|r=0\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\psi(W;\theta^{*},f_{\eta,n}\circ h_{m}+r(f_{\eta}\circ h-f_{\eta,n}\circ h_{m}))]\right|_{r=0}=𝔼ta​[{(fγ,n∘hm​(Z)−fγ∘h​(Z))−(fα,n∘hm​(Z)−fα∘h​(Z))​θ∗}​(X−fα,n∘hm​(Z))]\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\{(f_{\gamma,n}\circ h_{m}(Z)-f_{\gamma}\circ h(Z))-(f_{\alpha,n}\circ h_{m}(Z)-f_{\alpha}\circ h(Z))\theta^{*}\}(X-f_{\alpha,n}\circ h_{m}(Z))]+𝔼ta​[{(Y−fγ,n∘hm​(Z))−(X−fα,n∘hm​(Z))​θ∗}​(fα,n∘hm​(Z)−fα∘h​(Z))].\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{ta}}[\{(Y-f_{\gamma,n}\circ h_{m}(Z))-(X-f_{\alpha,n}\circ h_{m}(Z))\theta^{*}\}(f_{\alpha,n}\circ h_{m}(Z)-f_{\alpha}\circ h(Z))].

By the law of iterated expectations, the derivative equals zero in our setup by taking conditional expectation givenZZ.
Without these conditional-mean sufficiency restrictions, orthogonality with respect to the second-step regressions does not generally eliminate the direct evaluation effect induced by perturbing the generated regressor. This is the effect addressed by the first-step correction inEscanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026).242424WhileEscanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026)propose debiasing estimators for lower-dimensional generated regressors, debiasing for high-dimensional generated regressors is not studied in the literature.Our setup is the DML analog of the index restriction in the literature on semiparametric models (Mammenet al.,2016; Hahn and Ridder,2013).

When the embedding sufficiency is approximate (ϵta,n>0\epsilon_{\operatorname{ta},n}>0), Neyman orthogonality remains defined at the true nuisance vectorη∗\eta^{*}, rather than at its approximationfη,n∘hmf_{\eta,n}\circ h_{m}. Approximation and source-task estimation errors enter through the total errors of the composite nuisance estimators. Standard DML inference therefore remains valid when these total errors satisfy the required product-rate and sample-splitting conditions. In this sense, the two approaches are complementary. Our analysis derives source-to-target convergence rates for independently pre-trained embeddings, whereasEscanciano and Pérez-Izquierdo (2026)construct influence-function corrections for direct first-step effects that are not absorbed by orthogonality of the composite nuisance.

## Appendix BPre-trained Deep Learning

## B.1Fully Connected Feedforward Neural Networks (FNNs)

Let us consider a fully connected feedforward neural network withLNNL_{\mathrm{NN}}layers. The input layer is denoted as layer0, and the output layer is layerLNNL_{\mathrm{NN}}. Each layerℓ\ellhasHℓH_{\ell}hidden units (or neurons), and the output dimension isTT. The weights connecting layerℓ−1\ell-1to layerℓ\ellare represented by a weight matrixW(ℓ)∈ℝHℓ×Hℓ−1W^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{H_{\ell}\times H_{\ell-1}}, whereH0=dH_{0}=dandHLNN=TH_{L_{\mathrm{NN}}}=T. The constant terms (“biases”) for layerℓ\ellare represented by a vectorb(ℓ)∈ℝHℓb^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{H_{\ell}}.
Letσ​(⋅)\sigma(\cdot)denote an element-wise activation function, such as the ReLUσ​(a)=max⁡{a,0}\sigma(a)=\max\{a,0\}. To avoid confusion with the target functionsaso∗a_{\operatorname{so}}^{*}andata∗a_{\operatorname{ta}}^{*}used elsewhere in the paper, write the layer states asu(0)​(Z)=Zu^{(0)}(Z)=Z

and, forℓ=1,…,LNN−1\ell=1,\ldots,L_{\mathrm{NN}}-1,u(ℓ)​(Z)=σ​(W(ℓ)​u(ℓ−1)​(Z)+b(ℓ)).u^{(\ell)}(Z)=\sigma\left(W^{(\ell)}u^{(\ell-1)}(Z)+b^{(\ell)}\right).

If the economist uses the last hidden layer as the embedding, the embedding and source head in the notation of the main text arehm​(Z):=u(LNN−1)​(Z)∈ℝHLNN−1h_{m}(Z):=u^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}}-1)}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{H_{L_{\mathrm{NN}}-1}}

andgm​(v):=W(LNN)​v+b(LNN)∈ℝT.g_{m}(v):=W^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}})}v+b^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}})}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}.

Our theory is also applicable to other choices of the embeddings.
For example, the economist may use the second-to-last layer as the embedding, in which casehm​(Z):=u(LNN−2)​(Z)∈ℝHLNN−2h_{m}(Z):=u^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}}-2)}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{H_{L_{\mathrm{NN}}-2}}

andgm​(v):=W(LNN)​σ​(W(LNN−1)​v+b(LNN−1))+b(LNN)∈ℝT.g_{m}(v):=W^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}})}\sigma\left(W^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}}-1)}v+b^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}}-1)}\right)+b^{(L_{\mathrm{NN}})}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}.

## B.2Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs)

CNNs are designed for grid-structured inputs such as images. LetZ∈ℝHimg×Wimg×CimgZ\in\mathbb{R}^{H_{\mathrm{img}}\times W_{\mathrm{img}}\times C_{\mathrm{img}}}denote an image with heightHimgH_{\mathrm{img}}, widthWimgW_{\mathrm{img}}, andCimgC_{\mathrm{img}}channels (e.g., RGB color scale). A CNN replaces dense linear maps by local convolutional filters and pooling operations, so that early layers detect local patterns and deeper layers aggregate them into more abstract features. Denote byhm​(Z)∈ℝKh_{m}(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}

the output of the last pooling layer or penultimate layer. The remaining fully connected classification layer defines the source-task headgmg_{m}. Therefore, a pre-trained CNN, such as VGG19 (Simonyan and Zisserman,2015) or ResNet50 (Heet al.,2016), again has the formgm∘hmg_{m}\circ h_{m}. In applications, economists typically extracthwidecheck​(Zi)\widecheck{h}(Z_{i})from the final pooling layer and use these learned embeddings as regressors in the target task.
Alternatively, the economist may use the last hidden layer as the embedding, in which case the source headgmg_{m}includes the final activation function and the final classification layer.
Since the latter choice of embedding requires weaker assumptions for transferability, we recommend using the last hidden layer as the embedding in practice if the economist is indifferent between the two choices.

## B.3Transformer (BERT)

BERT is a bidirectional transformer encoder pre-trained on large text corpora (Devlinet al.,2019). Its original pre-training combines masked language modeling (MLM) and next sentence prediction (NSP). This structure is useful here because it makes the shared-embedding structure explicit. A packed BERT input has the formZ=([CLS],span A,[SEP],span B,[SEP]),Z=(\texttt{[CLS]},\ \text{span A},\ \texttt{[SEP]},\ \text{span B},\ \texttt{[SEP]}),

where the two spans may represent either a genuine neighboring pair or an artificially constructed pair. BERT tokenizes text into WordPiece units with a vocabulary size ofT≈30,000T\approx 30{,}000. Ifppdenotes a token position in the packed sequence, the input to the encoder isum(0)​(Z,p)=etok​(zp)+eseg​(sp)+epos​(p),u_{m}^{(0)}(Z,p)=e_{\mathrm{tok}}(z_{p})+e_{\mathrm{seg}}(s_{p})+e_{\mathrm{pos}}(p),

whereetoke_{\mathrm{tok}},esege_{\mathrm{seg}}, andepose_{\mathrm{pos}}denote token, segment, and
position embeddings, respectively.
LetLtrL_{\mathrm{tr}}denote the number of transformer blocks. LetZ=(z1,…,zM)Z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{M})denote a tokenized sequence of lengthMM. For each blockℓ\elland token positionpp, letum(ℓ)​(Z,p)∈ℝKu_{m}^{(\ell)}(Z,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}denote the hidden state at positionppafter blockℓ\ellin the fitted source encoder.
The transformer encoder maps{um(0)​(Z,p)}p=1M\{u_{m}^{(0)}(Z,p)\}_{p=1}^{M}into contextualized states{um(Ltr)​(Z,p)}p=1M\{u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,p)\}_{p=1}^{M}.

For MLM, letℳ​(Z)⊂{1,…,M}\mathcal{M}(Z)\subset\{1,\ldots,M\}denote the masked positions and letZcorrZ^{\mathrm{corr}}be the corrupted sequence used as input. The MLM head is a shallow neural network classifiergMLM,m:ℝK→ℝT,g_{\mathrm{MLM},m}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}^{T},

applied to each contextualized token stateum(Ltr)​(Zcorr,p)u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z^{\mathrm{corr}},p)forp∈ℳ​(Z)p\in\mathcal{M}(Z). LetSBERT=({Sp}p∈ℳ​(Z),SNSP)S^{\mathrm{BERT}}=\left(\{S_{p}\}_{p\in\mathcal{M}(Z)},S_{\mathrm{NSP}}\right)

collect the MLM and NSP labels for one sequence. IfSpS_{p}denotes the original token at a masked positionpp, the MLM loss for one sequence isℓMLM​(Z,SBERT)=−∑p∈ℳ​(Z)log⁡Pr⁡(Sp∣Zcorr,p).\ell_{\mathrm{MLM}}(Z,S^{\mathrm{BERT}})=-\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}(Z)}\log\Pr(S_{p}\mid Z^{\mathrm{corr}},p).

For NSP, the head is a binary classifiergNSP,m:ℝK→ℝ2,g_{\mathrm{NSP},m}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

applied to the[CLS]stateum(Ltr)​(Z,[CLS])u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,\texttt{[CLS]}). IfSNSP∈{0,1}S_{\mathrm{NSP}}\in\{0,1\}indicates whether span B truly follows span A, andπNSP​(Z)=softmax​(gNSP,m​(um(Ltr)​(Z,[CLS]))),\pi_{\mathrm{NSP}}(Z)=\texttt{softmax}(g_{\mathrm{NSP},m}(u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,\texttt{[CLS]}))),

then the NSP loss isℓNSP​(Z,SBERT)=−SNSP​log⁡πNSP,1​(Z)−(1−SNSP)​log⁡πNSP,0​(Z).\ell_{\mathrm{NSP}}(Z,S^{\mathrm{BERT}})=-S_{\mathrm{NSP}}\log\pi_{\mathrm{NSP},1}(Z)-(1-S_{\mathrm{NSP}})\log\pi_{\mathrm{NSP},0}(Z).

The overall BERT pre-training loss is thereforeℓBERT​(Z,SBERT)=ℓMLM​(Z,SBERT)+ℓNSP​(Z,SBERT).\ell_{\mathrm{BERT}}(Z,S^{\mathrm{BERT}})=\ell_{\mathrm{MLM}}(Z,S^{\mathrm{BERT}})+\ell_{\mathrm{NSP}}(Z,S^{\mathrm{BERT}}).

From the perspective of our theory, the important feature is that both pre-training tasks share the same encoder. The contextualized states{um(Ltr)​(Z,p)}p=1M\{u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,p)\}_{p=1}^{M}provide the common embedding, while the MLM and NSP prediction layers are task-specific heads. Hence BERT can be viewed as a pre-trained model with a shared embedding maphmh_{m}and a vector-valued source taskgmg_{m}. In downstream econometric applications, a common choice is either the[CLS]embeddinghwidecheck​(Z)=um(Ltr)​(Z,[CLS])\widecheck{h}(Z)=u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,\texttt{[CLS]})

or the pooled token embeddinghwidecheck​(Z)=1M​∑p=1Mum(Ltr)​(Z,p).\widecheck{h}(Z)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{p=1}^{M}u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,p).

The economist then treatshwidecheck​(Z)\widecheck{h}(Z)as the generated regressor in the target sample and estimates the downstream modelf∘hwidecheckf\circ\widecheck{h}.252525The original BERT paper fine-tunes the full network for the target task. In this paper, unless stated otherwise, we treat the pre-trained encoder as fixed and analyze the target estimator conditional on using the learned embeddinghwidecheck\widecheck{h}.Alternatively, as with CNNs, the economist may use the average of the last hidden layer outputs for the MLM task as the embedding, in which case the source headgmg_{m}includes the final activation function and the final classification layer.
Our recommendation is to use the last hidden layer as the embedding:hwidecheck​(Z)=1M​∑p=1MuMLM∘um(Ltr)​(Z,p),\widecheck{h}(Z)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{p=1}^{M}u_{\mathrm{MLM}}\circ u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,p),

whereuMLMu_{\mathrm{MLM}}denotes the transformation fromum(Ltr)​(Z,p)u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,p)to the MLM head input.

Across fully connected networks, CNNs, and transformers, the same structure recurs: a shared embedding maphmh_{m}is learned in the source task, a task-specific headgmg_{m}is attached to this embedding to predict source labels, and the estimated embeddinghwidecheck\widecheck{h}is carried to the target sample. This common decomposition is what makes the transfer-learning framework in the main text directly applicable to standard pre-trained deep learning models.

## Appendix CTransferability Condition for Deep Learning Models

In this section, we examine the transferability condition for CNNs and transformers.
This appendix explains how the sufficient conditions inTheorems˜3and1translate into common pre-trained deep learning models. The main message is that transferability depends on how informative the source-task head is about the learned embedding. When the last source head is linear, its slope matrix has enough rank, and the target class satisfies the required functional class condition, the sufficient conditions follow fromCorollary˜1. When the slope matrix is low-rank or the head is nonlinear, transferability can hold only for target tasks that depend on the embedding through the part preserved by the source outputs.

## C.1Transferability Condition for Pre-trained CNN

For a standard CNN classifier, the last source head is linear:gm​(v)=Wcnn,m​v+bcnn,m,g_{m}(v)=W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}v+b_{\mathrm{cnn},m},

wherev∈ℝKv\in\mathbb{R}^{K}is the last pooling or penultimate embedding,Wcnn,m∈ℝT×KW_{\mathrm{cnn},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}, andbcnn,m∈ℝTb_{\mathrm{cnn},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}. This includes the empirically common case in which the computer scientist trains the CNN on a multi-class image-classification task and the economist uses the frozen final pooling layer as the downstream embedding.

Suppose that the target model classℱn=ℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}=\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}is Lipschitz with constantLℱL_{\mathcal{F}}and thatAssumption˜3holds. IfWcnn,mW_{\mathrm{cnn},m}has full column rank, thenWcnn,m+=(Wcnn,m′​Wcnn,m)−1​Wcnn,m′W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{+}=(W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{\prime}W_{\mathrm{cnn},m})^{-1}W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{\prime}

is well defined, and for every target modelfnf_{n}we can defineΓ​(x)=fn​(Wcnn,m+​(x−bcnn,m)).\Gamma(x)=f_{n}\left(W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{+}(x-b_{\mathrm{cnn},m})\right).

ThenΓ∘gm∘hm​(Z)=fn​(Wcnn,m+​(Wcnn,m​hm​(Z)+bcnn,m−bcnn,m))=fn∘hm​(Z),\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z)=f_{n}\left(W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{+}(W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}h_{m}(Z)+b_{\mathrm{cnn},m}-b_{\mathrm{cnn},m})\right)=f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z),

and the Lipschitz constant ofΓ\Gammais bounded byLΓ≤Lℱ​‖Wcnn,m+‖sp.L_{\Gamma}\leq L_{\mathcal{F}}\|W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{+}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}.

Therefore, byCorollary˜1, the CNN source task is(ρso,m,ρta,n)(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n})-diverse overfnf_{n}withρta,n=Lℱσmin+​(Wcnn,m)​w¯n​ρso,m,\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=\frac{L_{\mathcal{F}}}{\sigma_{\min}^{+}(W_{\mathrm{cnn},m})\underline{w}_{n}}\rho_{\operatorname{so},m},

provided that the functional class requirement inCorollary˜1is satisfied. Specifically, for every candidate embeddinghhand corresponding best linear source headbcnn,m∙+Wcnn,m∙​(⋅)b_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{\bullet}+W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{\bullet}(\cdot), the target class must contain the functionu↦fn​(Wcnn,m+​(bcnn,m∙−bcnn,m+Wcnn,m∙​u)).u\mapsto f_{n}\left(W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{+}(b_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{\bullet}-b_{\mathrm{cnn},m}+W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{\bullet}u)\right).

The bias difference accounts for translations of the candidate embedding that can be absorbed by the source intercept. This functional class condition holds, for example, when the target class is closed under the displayed linear transformations with intercept shifts. Transferability is stronger when the smallest singular value ofWcnn,mW_{\mathrm{cnn},m}is bounded away from zero, because then‖Wcnn,m+‖sp\|W_{\mathrm{cnn},m}^{+}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}is small.

IfWcnn,mW_{\mathrm{cnn},m}is rank deficient, then universal transferability fails in general. When the target subclass consists of linear heads and the other conditions ofTheorem˜4hold,(0,0)(0,0)-diversity can hold only if the target coefficient belongs to the row span ofWcnn,mW_{\mathrm{cnn},m}. More generally,Theorem˜5implies that transferability requires the existence of a measurable mapΓ\Gammasuch thatfn∘hm​(Z)=Γ∘gm∘hm​(Z),f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)=\Gamma\circ g_{m}\circ h_{m}(Z),

PtaP_{\operatorname{ta}}-a.s. Hence, a CNN with a wide hidden embedding and relatively few source labels cannot be expected to transfer to arbitrary downstream targets; it transfers only to targets that depend on the embedding through the information preserved by the source classifier.

## C.2Transferability Condition for Pre-trained Transformer

For BERT-style transformers, the relevant source tasks depend on how the downstream embedding is constructed and on the fact that pre-training is carried out on a corrupted sequenceZ~=C​(Z,R)\widetilde{Z}=C(Z,R)rather than on the original sequenceZZ. Two common choices are the[CLS]embedding and the mean-pooled token embedding described inAppendix˜B. The main distinction is that the next sentence prediction task is a single sequence-level task, whereas masked language modeling generates many token-level source tasks.

## [CLS]embedding.

Suppose that the economist useshm​(Z)=um(Ltr)​(Z,[CLS]).h_{m}(Z)=u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,\texttt{[CLS]}).

If the only relevant source task is next sentence prediction, then the source head is linear of the formgNSP,m​(v)=WNSP,m​v+bNSP,m,g_{\mathrm{NSP},m}(v)=W_{\mathrm{NSP},m}v+b_{\mathrm{NSP},m},

whereWNSP,m∈ℝ2×KW_{\mathrm{NSP},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times K}. The full-rank sufficient condition inCorollary˜1can then hold only ifrank⁡(WNSP,m)=K\operatorname{rank}(W_{\mathrm{NSP},m})=K, which requiresK≤2K\leq 2. For empirically relevant transformer embeddings such as BERT,KKis much larger than22. Therefore, next sentence prediction alone cannot generically identify a high-dimensional[CLS]embedding. Transferability based only on this binary source task can hold only for target functions that depend onhm​(Z)h_{m}(Z)through the low-dimensional information preserved bygNSP,m∘hm​(Z)g_{\mathrm{NSP},m}\circ h_{m}(Z).

## Mean-pooled token embedding.

The more favorable case is when the economist uses a pooled embedding built from the token-level states relevant for masked language modeling. The BERT architecture may insert a small transform layer before the final vocabulary projection. Letum​(Z,p)=uMLM,m∘um(Ltr)​(Z,p)∈ℝKu_{m}(Z,p)=u_{\mathrm{MLM},m}\circ u_{m}^{(L_{\mathrm{tr}})}(Z,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{K}

denote the token state after this optional MLM-specific transform. If the economist uses the mean-pooled token embedding, the relevant target-side representation ishm​(Z)=1M​∑p=1Mum​(Z,p).h_{m}(Z)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{p=1}^{M}u_{m}(Z,p).

The MLM head then takes the linear formgMLM,m​(v)=WMLM,m​v+bMLM,m,g_{\mathrm{MLM},m}(v)=W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}v+b_{\mathrm{MLM},m},

whereWMLM,m∈ℝT×KW_{\mathrm{MLM},m}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times K}. In BERT, this matrix is often tied to the input token-embedding matrix, but this does not change the linear form of the final projection. Because the same vocabulary projection is applied to each token position, averaging the token-level logits on the corrupted source input yields1M​∑p=1MgMLM,m​(um​(Z~,p))=WMLM,m​hm​(Z~)+bMLM,m,\frac{1}{M}\sum_{p=1}^{M}g_{\mathrm{MLM},m}(u_{m}(\widetilde{Z},p))=W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}h_{m}(\widetilde{Z})+b_{\mathrm{MLM},m},

wherehm​(Z~)=1M​∑p=1Mum​(Z~,p).h_{m}(\widetilde{Z})=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{p=1}^{M}u_{m}(\widetilde{Z},p).

Thus, for the pooled representation induced by the corrupted sequence, the relevant source head isg¯MLM,m​(v)=WMLM,m​v+bMLM,m.\overline{g}_{\mathrm{MLM},m}(v)=W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}v+b_{\mathrm{MLM},m}.

IfWMLM,mW_{\mathrm{MLM},m}has full column rank, then the full-rank sufficient condition inCorollary˜1applies tog¯MLM,m\overline{g}_{\mathrm{MLM},m}on the source side. In particular, for every Lipschitz target modelfnf_{n}in the function class, there existsΓ​(x)=fn​(WMLM,m+​(x−bMLM,m))\Gamma(x)=f_{n}\left(W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}^{+}(x-b_{\mathrm{MLM},m})\right)

such thatfn∘hm​(Z~)=Γ∘g¯MLM,m∘hm​(Z~),f_{n}\circ h_{m}(\widetilde{Z})=\Gamma\circ\overline{g}_{\mathrm{MLM},m}\circ h_{m}(\widetilde{Z}),

provided that the target class satisfies the functional class condition inCorollary˜1. In particular, for every candidate embeddinghhand corresponding best linear source headbMLM,m∙+WMLM,m∙​(⋅)b_{\mathrm{MLM},m}^{\bullet}+W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}^{\bullet}(\cdot), the target class must containu↦fn​(WMLM,m+​(bMLM,m∙−bMLM,m+WMLM,m∙​u)).u\mapsto f_{n}\left(W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}^{+}(b_{\mathrm{MLM},m}^{\bullet}-b_{\mathrm{MLM},m}+W_{\mathrm{MLM},m}^{\bullet}u)\right).

Under this functional class condition,ρta,n=Lℱσmin+​(WMLM,m)​w¯n​ρso,m.\rho_{\operatorname{ta},n}=\frac{L_{\mathcal{F}}}{\sigma_{\min}^{+}(W_{\mathrm{MLM},m})\underline{w}_{n}}\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}.

This condition is substantially more plausible than in the[CLS]-NSP case because the MLM task has a much larger output dimension, and thus the final vocabulary projection can carry richer information about the pooled embedding. The optional dense-GELU-layer-normalization block in the BERT prediction head is not an obstacle here, because it can be absorbed into the definition of the embedding mapumu_{m}. IfWMLM,mW_{\mathrm{MLM},m}is rank deficient, universal transferability again fails in general. In that case, only target functions that depend on the pooled embedding through the information preserved by the row span ofWMLM,mW_{\mathrm{MLM},m}can be expected to transfer.

One difficulty with transformers is that BERT pre-training is carried out on a random corruption of the clean sequence rather than on the clean sequence itself. Letℐpred⊆{1,…,M}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pred}}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,M\}denote the set of token positions eligible for masked language modeling, excluding positions occupied by the special tokens[CLS],[SEP], and[PAD]. Draw the entries of a mask vectorR=(R1,…,RM)R=(R_{1},\ldots,R_{M})independently across positionsp∈ℐpredp\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pred}}. For these positions, letRp=1R_{p}=1with probabilityπ\piandRp=0R_{p}=0otherwise, and setRp=0R_{p}=0forp∉ℐpredp\notin\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pred}}. In BERT,π=0.15\pi=0.15. Let𝒰​(R)={p∈ℐpred:Rp=1}\mathcal{U}(R)=\{p\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pred}}:R_{p}=1\}denote the set of masked positions. The corrupted sequenceZ~=C​(Z,R)\widetilde{Z}=C(Z,R)is then defined coordinatewise byZ~p={[MASK],p∈𝒰​(R)​with probability​0.8,Zprnd,p∈𝒰​(R)​with probability​0.1,Zp,p∈𝒰​(R)​with probability​0.1,Zp,p∉𝒰​(R),\widetilde{Z}_{p}=\begin{cases}\texttt{[MASK]},&p\in\mathcal{U}(R)\text{ with probability }0.8,\\
Z_{p}^{\mathrm{rnd}},&p\in\mathcal{U}(R)\text{ with probability }0.1,\\
Z_{p},&p\in\mathcal{U}(R)\text{ with probability }0.1,\\
Z_{p},&p\notin\mathcal{U}(R),\end{cases}

whereZprndZ_{p}^{\mathrm{rnd}}is drawn uniformly from the BERT vocabulary. For a fixed clean sequence and a masked position, averaging the source loss over this corruption distribution yields the masked conditional objective used in pre-training. Therefore, the observed MLM loss is an unbiased Monte Carlo approximation to that population criterion. The 80/10/10 rule also prevents the source task from depending only on the literal[MASK]token and thereby mitigates the train-target mismatch between masked source inputs and unmasked target inputs.

In our framework, the source task therefore identifies the representation throughhm​(Z~)h_{m}(\widetilde{Z})under the joint law induced by the clean source sequence and the corruption draw, whereas the economist useshm​(Z)h_{m}(Z)in the target sample. A concrete route to a positivew¯n\underline{w}_{n}comes from the fact that the clean sequence is observed with positive probability under the BERT corruption scheme. Indeed, each eligible token is left unchanged with probability1−0.9​π1-0.9\pi, sopM=Pr⁡(Z~=Z∣Z)≥(1−0.9​π)M>0.p_{M}=\Pr(\widetilde{Z}=Z\mid Z)\geq(1-0.9\pi)^{M}>0.

On the event{Z~=Z}\{\widetilde{Z}=Z\}, the pooled embedding is unchanged. Therefore, if the uncorrupted source distribution ofZZalready satisfies the density ratio condition inAssumption˜3, then the corrupted-source comparison above also holds with a constantw¯n\underline{w}_{n}equal to the clean-sample constant multiplied bypM1/2p_{M}^{1/2}. This bound is likely to be too conservative because it uses only the event that no effective corruption occurs. The mean-pooling structure suggests that a larger constant may be available under additional stability conditions on the transformer states, since many tokens remain unchanged and the average embedding can still preserve the target-relevant variation even when some positions are corrupted. Thus, the corruption operator need not remove the components of the pooled embedding that matter for the downstream econometric task, and when this comparison holds, the sufficient transferability arguments above continue to apply with the corrupted source covariateZ~\widetilde{Z}in place of the original unmasked sequence.

## Appendix ETechnical Lemmas

## Lemma 2(Transferability Decomposition).

Suppose thatℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}is a class of square-integrable functions and thatℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}is a class of measurable functions. For everyfn∈L2​(P)f_{n}\in L^{2}(P)and everyh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m},inff∈ℱnsub‖f∘h−fn∘hm‖P,22\displaystyle\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\|f\circ h-f_{n}\circ h_{m}\|_{P,2}^{2}=𝔼​[Var⁡(fn∘hm​(Z)∣h​(Z))]+inff∈ℱnsub𝔼​[(𝔼​[fn∘hm​(Z)∣h​(Z)]−f∘h​(Z))2].\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\left[\operatorname{Var}\left(f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)\mid h(Z)\right)\right]+\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}\left[f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)\mid h(Z)\right]-f\circ h(Z)\right)^{2}\right].

## Remark 6.

The first term on the right-hand side represents the variation offn∘hm​(Z)f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)that cannot be explained byh​(Z)h(Z); the second term represents the best approximation of the conditional expectation offn∘hm​(Z)f_{n}\circ h_{m}(Z)givenh​(Z)h(Z)by functions inℱnsub\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\operatorname{sub}}.
Our sufficient condition for transferability inTheorem˜3ensures that both terms are small whenh∈ℋso,m​(ρso,m)h\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(\rho_{\operatorname{so},m}).
Our necessary conditions inTheorems˜5and4make the first term equal to zero for someh∈ℋso,m​(0)h\in\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{so},m}(0).Example˜10illustrates the importance of the second term as it can be positive even when the first term is zero, which leads to failure of transferability.

## Lemma 3(Doob-Dynkin Factorization under Completion).

Let(Ω,𝒜,P)(\Omega,\mathcal{A},P)be a probability space, letU:Ω→𝒰U:\Omega\to\mathcal{U}be a random element taking values in a measurable space, and letV:Ω→ℝqV:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{q}be aqq-dimensional random vector. Letσ​(U)¯P\overline{\sigma(U)}^{P}be thePP-completion ofσ​(U)\sigma(U), which is defined byσ​(U)¯P={A∪N:A∈σ​(U),N⊂B​for some​B∈σ​(U)​with​P​(B)=0}\overline{\sigma(U)}^{P}=\{A\cup N:A\in\sigma(U),N\subset B\text{ for some }B\in\sigma(U)\text{ with }P(B)=0\}.
Ifσ​(V)⊆σ​(U)¯P,\sigma(V)\subseteq\overline{\sigma(U)}^{P},

then there exists a measurable functionΓ:𝒰→ℝq\Gamma:\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}such thatV=Γ∘UV=\Gamma\circ U,PP-a.s.

Suppose that, for some constantM>0M>0and all candidate functionsffandaa,supz∈𝒵‖f​(z)‖2,supz∈𝒵‖a​(z)‖2,supz∈𝒵‖f∗​(z)‖2≤M.\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\|f(z)\|_{2},\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\|a(z)\|_{2},\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\|f^{*}(z)\|_{2}\leq M.

Then,cL=12​(1+T​exp⁡(M))2,cU=exp⁡(M)2​(1+(T−1)​exp⁡(−M)+exp⁡(M)),Cℓ=2c_{L}=\frac{1}{2(1+T\exp(M))^{2}},\qquad c_{U}=\frac{\exp(M)}{2(1+(T-1)\exp(-M)+\exp(M))},\qquad C_{\ell}=\sqrt{2}

satisfycL​‖f−f∗‖P,22≤𝔼​[ℓ​(f​(Z),Y)]−𝔼​[ℓ​(f∗​(Z),Y)]≤cU​‖f−f∗‖P,22c_{L}\|f-f^{*}\|_{P,2}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\ell(f(Z),Y)]-\mathbb{E}[\ell(f^{*}(Z),Y)]\leq c_{U}\|f-f^{*}\|_{P,2}^{2}

for all candidate functionsffandaa, everyz∈𝒵z\in\mathcal{Z}, and everyy∈{1,…,T+1}y\in\{1,\ldots,T+1\}.

## Remark 7.

For fixedM>0M>0,cL≍T−2c_{L}\asymp T^{-2}asT→∞T\to\infty, and this order cannot generally be improved. To see this, takeZZto be deterministic andf∗​(z)=𝟎T∈ℝTf^{*}(z)=\mathbf{0}_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}, so thatP​(Y=t∣Z)=1/(T+1)P(Y=t\mid Z)=1/(T+1)fort=1,…,T+1t=1,\ldots,T+1. LetδT=M​T−1/2\delta_{T}=MT^{-1/2}and choosef​(z)=δT​𝟏Tf(z)=\delta_{T}\mathbf{1}_{T}, which satisfies‖f−f∗‖P,22=M2\|f-f^{*}\|_{P,2}^{2}=M^{2}andsupz∈𝒵‖f​(z)‖2=M\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\|f(z)\|_{2}=M. Then,𝔼​[ℓ​(f​(Z),Y)]−𝔼​[ℓ​(f∗​(Z),Y)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\ell(f(Z),Y)]-\mathbb{E}[\ell(f^{*}(Z),Y)]=−T​δTT+1+log⁡(1+T​exp⁡(δT))−log⁡(1+T)\displaystyle=-\frac{T\delta_{T}}{T+1}+\log(1+T\exp(\delta_{T}))-\log(1+T)=δTT+1+log⁡(1+exp⁡(−δT)−1T+1)\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{T}}{T+1}+\log\left(1+\frac{\exp(-\delta_{T})-1}{T+1}\right)=δT+exp⁡(−δT)−1T+1+o​(T−2)\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{T}+\exp(-\delta_{T})-1}{T+1}+o(T^{-2})=M22​T​(T+1)+o​(T−2)≍T−2,\displaystyle=\frac{M^{2}}{2T(T+1)}+o(T^{-2})\asymp T^{-2},

where the third equality useslog⁡(1+x)=x+O​(x2)\log(1+x)=x+O(x^{2})and the fourth equality usesexp⁡(−x)=1−x+x2/2+o​(x2)\exp(-x)=1-x+x^{2}/2+o(x^{2})asx→0x\to 0.

For a norm∥⋅∥\|\cdot\|andε>0\varepsilon>0, letN(ε,ℱ,∥⋅∥)N\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|\right)denote the covering number ofℱ\mathcal{F}, i.e., the minimum number of balls of radiusε\varepsilonwith respect to the norm∥⋅∥\|\cdot\|needed to cover the setℱ\mathcal{F}.
The following two lemmas are also proved as part of the proof of Theorem 7 inTripuraneniet al.(2020), but we state them here for completeness and clarity.

## Lemma 6(Properties of Products of Covering Numbers).

Letℋ\mathcal{H}be a measurable class of functions withKK-dimensional outputs. Suppose thatℋk\mathcal{H}_{k}is thekk-th coordinate projection ofℋ\mathcal{H}, i.e.,ℋk={hk:h=(h1,…,hK)∈ℋ}\mathcal{H}_{k}=\{h_{k}:h=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{K})\in\mathcal{H}\}. Then, we haveN​(ε,ℋ,L2​(Q))≤∏k=1KN​(ε/K,ℋk,L2​(Q)).N\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{H},L^{2}(Q)\right)\leq\prod_{k=1}^{K}N\left(\varepsilon/K,\mathcal{H}_{k},L^{2}(Q)\right).

## Lemma 7(Properties of Composition of Covering Numbers).

Letℱ\mathcal{F}andℋ\mathcal{H}be measurable classes of functions. Suppose thatℱ\mathcal{F}is Lipschitz with constantLℱL_{\mathcal{F}}. Then, we haveN​(ε,ℱ∘ℋ,L2​(Q))≤N​(ε/(2​Lℱ),ℋ,L2​(Q))⋅suph′∈ℋN​(ε/2,ℱ,L2​(Qh′)),N\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{H},L^{2}(Q)\right)\leq N\left(\varepsilon/(2L_{\mathcal{F}}),\mathcal{H},L^{2}(Q)\right)\cdot\sup_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}N\left(\varepsilon/2,\mathcal{F},L^{2}(Q_{h^{\prime}})\right),

whereQh′Q_{h^{\prime}}is the measure such thatQh′​(A)=Q​(h′⁣−1​(A))Q_{h^{\prime}}(A)=Q(h^{\prime-1}(A))for any measurable setAA.

## Definition 5(Uniform Entropy Integral).

For a classℱ\mathcal{F}of measurable functions with measurable envelope functionFF, define theuniform entropy integralbyJ​(δ,ℱ|F,L2)=supQ∫0δ1+log⁡N​(ε​‖F‖Q,2,ℱ,L2​(Q))​𝑑ε,J\left(\delta,\mathcal{F}|F,L_{2}\right)=\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{1+\log N\left(\varepsilon\|F\|_{Q,2},\mathcal{F},L_{2}(Q)\right)}d\varepsilon,

where the supremum is taken over all discrete probability measuresQQwith‖F‖Q,2>0\|F\|_{Q,2}>0.

Importantly, the uniform entropy integral depends only on the function class and its domain, not on a particular probability measure.

Letℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}be a pointwise measurable class of measurable functions mapping intoℝT\mathbb{R}^{T}withsupv‖f​(v)‖2≤M<∞\sup_{v}\|f(v)\|_{2}\leq M<\inftyfor everyf∈ℱnf\in\mathcal{F}_{n}, letℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}be a class of measurable functions, and leth↦fn,h∗∈ℱnh\mapsto f_{n,h}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}be a mapping.

and, for everyh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m}andδ>0\delta>0, definedn,h​(f,f′)=‖f∘h−f′∘h‖P,2d_{n,h}(f,f^{\prime})=\|f\circ h-f^{\prime}\circ h\|_{P,2},Bn,h​(δ)={f∈ℱn:dn,h​(f,fn,h∗)<δ}B_{n,h}(\delta)=\left\{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}:d_{n,h}\left(f,f_{n,h}^{*}\right)<\delta\right\},
andℳn,h​(δ)={mf,h−mfn,h∗,h:f∈Bn,h​(δ)}\mathcal{M}_{n,h}(\delta)=\left\{m_{f,h}-m_{f_{n,h}^{*},h}:f\in B_{n,h}(\delta)\right\}.

Suppose thatℓ:ℝT×𝒴↦ℝ\ell:\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathcal{Y}\mapsto\mathbb{R}is Lipschitz continuous in the first argument with respect to∥⋅∥2\|\cdot\|_{2}with constantCℓC_{\ell}.
Then,suph∈ℋm𝔼P​[supf∈Bn,h​(δ)n​|(𝕄n−Mn)​(f,h)−(𝕄n−Mn)​(fn,h∗,h)|]\displaystyle\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{m}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\sup_{f\in B_{n,h}(\delta)}\sqrt{n}\left|\left(\mathbb{M}_{n}-M_{n}\right)(f,h)-\left(\mathbb{M}_{n}-M_{n}\right)\left(f_{n,h}^{*},h\right)\right|\right]≲Cℓ​M​(J​(δ/M,ℱn|M,L2)+M2​J2​(δ/M,ℱn|M,L2)δ2​n).\displaystyle\quad\lesssim C_{\ell}M\left(J\left(\delta/M,\mathcal{F}_{n}|M,L_{2}\right)+\frac{M^{2}J^{2}\left(\delta/M,\mathcal{F}_{n}|M,L_{2}\right)}{\delta^{2}\sqrt{n}}\right).

## Lemma 9(Localized Pointwise Measurable Class).

LetT≥1T\geq 1and letℱ\mathcal{F}be a pointwise measurable class of measurable functions mapping intoℝT\mathbb{R}^{T}with measurable envelope functionFFsuch that‖f​(x)‖2≤F​(x)\|f(x)\|_{2}\leq F(x)for everyf∈ℱf\in\mathcal{F}andP​F2<∞PF^{2}<\infty.
Then, the localized classB​(δ)={f∈ℱ:‖f−f∗‖P,2<δ}B(\delta)=\{f\in\mathcal{F}:\|f-f^{*}\|_{P,2}<\delta\}is also pointwise measurable for everyδ>0\delta>0and every measurable functionf∗f^{*}mapping intoℝT\mathbb{R}^{T}such that‖f∗​(x)‖2≤F​(x)\|f^{*}(x)\|_{2}\leq F(x).

## Lemma 10(Covering Number for VC Function Classes,van der Vaart and Wellner,2023, Theorem 2.6.7).

For a VC-class of functions with measurable envelope functionFFandr≥1r\geq 1, every probability measureQQwith‖F‖Q,r>0\|F\|_{Q,r}>0satisfiesN​(ε​‖F‖Q,r,ℱ,Lr​(Q))≲𝖵​(ℱ)​(16​e)𝖵​(ℱ)​(1ε)r​𝖵​(ℱ),N\left(\varepsilon\|F\|_{Q,r},\mathcal{F},L_{r}(Q)\right)\lesssim\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F})(16e)^{\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F})}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)^{r\mathsf{V}(\mathcal{F})},

for0<ε<10<\varepsilon<1.

## Remark 8.

The pointwise-measurability assumptions are required to ensure the measurability of the supremum within the expectations. We can eliminate this requirement by using outer expectations and probabilities instead (seevan der Vaart and Wellner,2023, Sections 1.2 and 2.3).

## Lemma 12(Rate of Convergence for Regularized Estimator).

For eachnn, letℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}be a set of measurable functions, letℱn∗\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*}be a set of measurable functions, and letℋm\mathcal{H}_{m}be a set of measurable functionshh.
Assume that for every fixedh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m}, there exist mappingsh↦fn,h∈ℱn,h↦fn,h∗∈ℱn∗.h\mapsto f_{n,h}\in\mathcal{F}_{n},\quad h\mapsto f_{n,h}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*}.

For eachnn, let𝕄n\mathbb{M}_{n}be a stochastic process and letMnM_{n}be a deterministic function indexed by a set(ℱn∪ℱn∗)×ℋm(\mathcal{F}_{n}\cup\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*})\times\mathcal{H}_{m}.
For every fixedh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m}, letf↦dn,h​(f,fn,h∗)f\mapsto d_{n,h}(f,f_{n,h}^{*})be a deterministic function fromℱn\mathcal{F}_{n}to[0,∞)[0,\infty), and letδ¯n≥0\underline{\delta}_{n}\geq 0,τn∈(0,1]\tau_{n}\in(0,1], andλn>0\lambda_{n}>0be some sequences.
Let𝒥n:ℱn→[0,∞)\mathcal{J}_{n}:\mathcal{F}_{n}\rightarrow[0,\infty)be some complexity measure offf.

## Lemma 13(Curvature Condition for Approximate Maximizer).

Letdn,h​(⋅,⋅)d_{n,h}(\cdot,\cdot)be a norm on a set containingℱn∪ℱn∗\mathcal{F}_{n}\cup\mathcal{F}_{n}^{*}for every fixedh∈ℋmh\in\mathcal{H}_{m}.
